# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-25

## LLM Agents
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[EpiSEM-Bench: Evaluating Safety and Evidence Recovery in LLM Agents under Missing Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31650v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using language model agents rely on environmental facts to decide whether an action is permitted. When a tool response omits a safety-relevant fact, an agent may carry out a prohibited action even though the user request is benign and every returned statement is true. We introduce \bench{}, an executable benchmark for evaluating unsafe execution, safety boundaries, and evidence recovery in LLM agents under missing evidence. It contains 300 matched unsafe/safe task pairs across six domains. By systematically suppressing critical facts, the benchmark measures unsafe execution under critical evidence omission and determines the minimum observed failure cardinality, including failures with full evidence and cases with no observed failure after exhaustive testing. An independent audit tool tests whether agents seek missing facts and then withhold prohibited actions while completing permissible tasks. The benchmark reveals substantial variation across the ten models in omission robustness, evidence acquisition, and appropriate action. Resistance to missing evidence does not necessarily translate into useful recovery. Recovery access reduces unsafe execution for all ten models, but agents differ in whether they actively obtain the missing facts and use them to act appropriately in both worlds.</p></details> |  |
| **[ATTUNE: Agentic Throughput Tuning for LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17528v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The throughput of an LLM training job depends on its recipe: how the model is split across GPUs, how memory is traded for compute, and which kernels and communication overlaps it uses. The best recipe changes with every model, accelerator and framework version. Automated tuners search a fixed space of knobs and check only that a job runs, so they miss levers outside that space and can ship a configuration that silently changes training. ATTUNE is a coding agent that tunes any working recipe unattended within a GPU-hour budget. Guided by a skill of search rules, scripts and lessons from past tuning campaigns, it diagnoses each run and changes one lever at a time. A correctness gate outside the agent refuses any argument known to change the training objective. ATTUNE tunes Amazon's production post-training recipes on H100, H200 and B200 clusters, raising throughput by 1.7x on average. It beats the published throughput of four of NVIDIA's own benchmark recipes on NVIDIA's AutoModel framework by 6-17%. On models held out from its development, it improves the strongest public recipes most where they were written for another setting, by up to 2.6x on MiniMax-M2, and least where the vendor tuned them for the same hardware. Given the same GPU-hour budget, it outperforms existing automated tuners, including Bayesian optimization, random search and OptiCo, a recent LLM-agent tuner. Its gain on GLM-4.5 is more than four times the best alternative's. The same coding agent without ATTUNE's skill gains less and fails half its launches. We release ATTUNE at https://github.com/amazon-science/attune and OpenRecipes, a leaderboard of tuned and verified training recipes, at https://openrecipes.ai.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 2 figures. v2: adds NVIDIA NeMo AutoModel benchmark recipes, production deployment results and the OpenRecipes leaderboard</p></details> |
| **[TabAgent: Distilling LLM Agent Components into Cheap Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16429v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems, AI architectures that autonomously execute multi-step workflows to achieve complex goals, are often built using repeated large language model (LLM) calls for closed-set decision tasks such as routing, shortlisting, gating, and verification. While convenient, this design makes deployments slow and expensive due to cumulative latency and token usage. We propose TabAgent, a framework for replacing generative decision components in closed-set selection tasks with a compact textual-tabular classifier trained on execution traces. TabAgent (i) extracts structured schema, state, and dependency features from trajectories (TabSchema), (ii) augments coverage with schema-aligned synthetic supervision (TabSynth), and (iii) scores candidates with a lightweight classifier (TabHead). On the long-horizon AppWorld benchmark, TabAgent maintains task-level success while eliminating shortlist-time LLM calls, reducing latency by approximately 95% and inference cost by 85-91%. Beyond tool shortlisting, TabAgent generalizes to other agentic decision heads, establishing a paradigm for learned discriminative replacements of generative bottlenecks in production agent architectures.</p></details> | <details><summary>67 pa...</summary><p>67 pages, 14 figures, 26 tables. v2 is a substantial revision with a new title and two added authors. The evaluation is extended from AppWorld to seven agent benchmarks across three decision families (set retrieval, classification, policy and reward), and TabHead is now a CatBoost classifier with an ablation over classical and neural heads</p></details> |
| **[Evaluating Rational Contracting in Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10475v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The emergence of language-based AI agents promises to transform the scope of machine economic activity. Instead of just proposing bids or following hard-coded protocols, such agents can be used to negotiate and execute agreements in natural language. However, most evaluations of these abilities have focused on one-off exchanges or simple economic games, leaving open the rich space of time-extended, contingent, and incomplete contracts expressible in language; they also focus on raw profit, without measuring the qualities required for trustworthy contracting. We address this by formulating a rational framework for how agents should negotiate and perform natural language contracts in uncertain multi-step environments. Using this framework, we develop metrics and baselines for quantifying rational and cooperative play. We instantiate our framework in ContractSim, an evaluation suite where two players negotiate and execute a multi-turn supplier contract under environmental and inter-player uncertainty. We find that LLM-based agents agree on efficient contracts when environmental uncertainty is low, but under high uncertainty, often fail to negotiate satisfiable, efficient, or mutually beneficial contracts, underperforming even heuristic baselines. When executing contracts, LLM agents are also frequently uncooperative, violating contracts for extra profit even when they are easy to satisfy. These findings highlight room for improvement in the design of language agents that can negotiate and execute contracts both rationally and cooperatively.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 7 figures, 2 tables (Appendix: 40 pages, 8 figures, 10 tables)</p></details> |
| **[MLEvolve: A Self-Evolving Framework for Automated Machine Learning Algorithm Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06473v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly applied to long-horizon tasks such as scientific discovery and machine learning engineering (MLE), where sustained self-evolution becomes a key capability. However, existing MLE agents suffer from isolated exploration, inefficient experience reuse, and lack of hierarchical control, which together hinder long-horizon optimization. We present MLEvolve, an LLM-based self-evolving multi-agent framework for end-to-end machine learning algorithm discovery. By extending tree search to Progressive MCGS, MLEvolve enables cross-branch information flow through graph-based reference edges and gradually shifts the search from broad exploration to focused exploitation with an entropy-inspired progressive schedule. To allow the agent to evolve with accumulated experience, we introduce Retrospective Memory, which combines a cold-start domain knowledge base with a dynamic global memory for task-specific experience retrieval and reuse. For stable long-horizon iteration, we further decouple strategic planning from code generation with adaptive coding modes. Evaluation on MLE-Bench shows that MLEvolve achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple dimensions including average medal rate and valid submission rate under a 12-hour budget (half the standard runtime). Moreover, MLEvolve also outperforms specialized algorithm discovery methods including AlphaEvolve on mathematical algorithm optimization tasks, demonstrating strong cross-domain generalization. Code is available at https://github.com/InternScience/MLEvolve.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 8 figures, 6 tables. Project page: https://internscience.github.io/MLEvolve/</p></details> |
| **[Semantics Delivery Network: Rethinking Web Retrieval Infrastructure for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22486v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly ground their answers in proprietary data and live web content through both single-shot retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and multi-turn agentic search. Yet the web path they use was built for human readers: search services return URLs and snippets ranked by generic relevance, and content delivery networks (CDNs) cache URL-addressed objects without knowing which passage an agent needs. LLMs instead consume short, semantically coherent passages - hereafter, chunks - selected for downstream utility rather than similarity alone, retrieve statefully across reasoning turns, and, as uncoordinated streams, repeatedly fetch, clean, chunk, and embed the same content. We argue that semantic chunk retrieval should become a first-class network-delivery abstraction, and propose the Semantics Delivery Network (SemDN): an origin-authorized, hierarchical substrate that indexes, searches, and caches web content at chunk granularity, serving clients and origins alike. SemDN is more than finer-grained caching: it must estimate missing or stale evidence without an explicit miss signal, let clients choose retrieval representations that origins need not see, and share state among clients with the same representation. Preliminary results show that page-first retrieval processes two to three orders of magnitude more content than agents consume, that agentic queries exhibit task-local chunk reuse invisible to URL caches, and that chunk delivery improves answer quality per context token. We organize an open research agenda around shareable retrieval state, hierarchical caching and refresh under coverage risk, and deployability, including incentives, privacy, and verifiability. Our code is available at: https://github.com/sarendis56/SemDN_HotNets26</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted and to appear at the 25th ACM Workshop on Hot Topics in Networks (HotNets 2026). 13 pages, 3 figures</p></details> |
| **[Training Small Language Models for Transferable Tool Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31483v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM agents operate over corpora of more than ten thousand tools, selecting the right tool for a complex multi-step user instruction becomes a central bottleneck. Tool descriptions cannot all fit in context, and relevance-based retrievers only rank tools without knowing which are actually needed. Recent work instead leverages proprietary commercial LLMs like Claude or ChatGPT to drive a retriever, decomposing the instruction and selecting over retrieved candidates. We show that a small, open model can be trained via reinforcement learning to perform the same functionality. On ToolBench, a Qwen3-8B model post-trained with our recipe outperforms Llama-4-Maverick (402B). In addition, the trained model transfers directly to different retrievers and generalizes to new tool corpora in different domains.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 5 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[TRCA: Transition-wise Rubric Credit Assignment for Long-horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16156v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon large language model (LLM) agents are typically optimized with sparse terminal outcomes, making fine-grained credit assignment across multi-step interactions difficult. Existing approaches either rely on process evaluators, which incur annotation and inference costs, or derive step-level credit from successful trajectories. However, successful trajectories are extremely scarce during early-stage reinforcement learning, substantially weakening anchor-based methods. We propose Transition-wise Rubric Credit Assignment (TRCA), which derives step-level supervision directly from action-induced transitions without learned evaluators or successful anchors. TRCA evaluates each transition using Evidence, Execution, and Invalidity rubrics to capture task-relevant information acquisition, valid task execution, and invalid or regressive behavior. From these judgments, Foundational Rubric Reward measures local transition quality, while Breakthrough Rubric Reward tracks newly covered Evidence and Execution conditions to reward incremental task progress. Combined with terminal outcomes, these signals produce fine-grained step-level advantages for policy optimization. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and seven search-augmented question-answering benchmarks show consistent improvements over the evaluated baselines. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, TRCA improves the WebShop score by 6.0%-12.6%; with Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct, it improves the average SearchQA score by 1.9%-18.3%. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of transition-wise rubric credit assignment for long-horizon tasks with sparse successful anchors.</p></details> |  |
| **[Certified Execution Harness for Agentic AI: Safe and Least-Intrusive Petri-Net Supervisory Control Between Reasoning and Action](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31376v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper studies the problem of safe tool execution for large language model (LLM) agents under prompt injection. Using Petri nets, we formalize the execution harness between the LLM and its tools as a supervisory control problem, in which marks represent capabilities, approvals and budgets. Since a transition fires only if the marks it needs are present, an injected request whose preconditions are unmet or already spent is refused regardless of its wording. Treating tool outcomes as uncontrollable, we enforce safety by the conventional maximally permissive supervisor, and this safety transfers to the running system under four assumptions. Moreover, to capture an agent that may propose again after a refusal, we introduce the notion of a replanning closed loop, in which each retry costs an inference call. We show that, in this loop, the conventional supervisor can be arbitrarily more intrusive than a stricter safe one. Furthermore, for a given proposer model, the maximally permissive least-intrusive supervisor is characterized as a value-threshold refinement of the conventional one. Under precise conditions, this refinement orders two concurrent actions by at most one monitor place. On an autonomous release agent, we find by synthesis that safe deployment requires a step that the supervisor can refuse after a rollout starts.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 9 figures, 2 tables. Extended version of a paper submitted for publication</p></details> |
| **[AfterDeleteBench: Auditing Post-Deletion Information Persistence in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31366v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many LLM agents maintain external persistent memory and derive additional state from past interactions, such as summaries. Deleting an original memory record therefore need not remove information carried by its descendants. We introduce AfterDeleteBench, a controlled behavioral audit for this failure mode in fixed-weight LLM agents. For each synthetic secret, we compare an exposed-then-deleted agent with an otherwise matched agent that was never exposed to the secret, allowing post-deletion recovery to be attributed to the original exposure. In our primary setting, a single query recovers the deleted secret from Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct in roughly two thirds of cases, while matched never-exposed controls have zero recovery. Crucially, recovery does not require a surviving verbatim copy: when the complete secret string is absent and only separately stored components remain under a public composition rule, exact reconstruction succeeds in 62-99% of cases across Qwen, Mistral, and Phi-3. We also show that the response interface is part of the measurement system: target-bearing replies can be scored as failures when the requested format and parser are misaligned. Finally, we separate reconstruction from write-back and fresh-session recall. Provenance-aware closure eliminates observed recovery in the tested positive-baseline settings, whereas an exact-literal write filter blocks reinsertion but cannot erase information already present. Together, these results show that source deletion should be evaluated behaviorally at the level of the agent and its memory system, not only as a storage operation.</p></details> | <details><summary>25 pa...</summary><p>25 pages, 7 figures, 12 tables</p></details> |
| **[Disclosure Begets Disclosure: A Self-Reinforcing Privacy Loop in LLM Agents with Persistent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28315v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly use persistent memory to personalize responses, and personal attributes in this memory pose a privacy risk if exposed. We ask how consulting memory during dialogue changes what users go on to say, and thus what the memory comes to reveal. In multi-session simulations, a simulated user with 24 personal attributes talks with a support agent that does or does not consult memory, and in both settings attributes are inferred from the full accumulated record. Across four support models, memory raises response quality but also empirical leakage, the share of attribute values correctly inferred from the record, by a margin that widens over sessions. The cause is a loop in ordinary, non-adversarial assistance. Memory-conditioned responses carry earlier values forward, often as presuppositions, and users affirm them or leave them uncorrected, so that cues and the agent's own guesses become recorded values. The loop reinforces itself, and removing the agent's mentions of an attribute lowers its later disclosure about twice as much as single-turn effects predict. Users cannot leave it on their own, since correcting a value discloses the attribute, declining to discuss it shifts later predictions toward sensitive values, and incorrect values left in place become entrenched. The loop is best weakened at the agent's response. Instructing the agent not to state or presuppose attributes brings leakage to about the no-memory level while keeping most of the quality gains, whereas filtering or anonymizing what users say or what is stored gives up much of them.</p></details> |  |
| **[Consistent but Biased: Chair Personas Trade Noise for Bias in Multi-Agent Clinical Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31325v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In clinical systems built from several large language model (LLM) agents, one agent often chairs the discussion and makes the final decision. Whether the chair's persona prompt changes that decision, or the agreement between repeated runs that some systems treat as reliability, has not been tested with the case held constant. We ask whether the persona changes the decision or only its timing, how it affects agreement between runs, and which behaviors appear when the prompt describes only personality traits. A simulated department of seven agents discussed 20 published cases of degenerative cervical myelopathy, with the case reports replayed unchanged in every run. Only the prompts of the chair and its deputy changed (no persona, or a consultative, autocratic or avoidant one), written either as rules of conduct or as personality traits. With personas written as rules, the chair chose to operate now in 93% of runs when autocratic and in 2% when avoidant, against 57% without a persona. Surgery was still planned in nearly all decisions, so the personas mainly changed whether to operate now or after more tests. These effects held with a second model and reworded prompts. The autocratic and avoidant personas made the three runs of a case agree more often, but only about as often as chance predicts, and Fleiss $κ$ did not rise. Prompts that described only personality traits kept more than half of the autocratic and avoidant effects on operating now. They reproduced quick decisions, delays and extra tests, but not ignoring red flags, dropping backup plans or passing the decision to the family. Studies should report and vary the chair's persona as they do the model, and correct agreement between runs for chance before calling it reliability.</p></details> | 32 pages, 8 figures |
| **[On the Behavioral Traits of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32776v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Users increasingly describe different AI agents as distinct colleagues to work with. AI personality research aims to quantify such impressions by attributing human- like "traits" to agents. However, existing measures fall short: models' self-reports (S-data) diverge from their actual behavior, while informant ratings from LLM judges (I-data) are costly to scale and cover few everyday scenarios. In this paper, we propose A-B-D to infer traits bottom-up from behavioral data (B-data), namely how agents act on their environment and communicate with users, as recorded in existing trajectories. From 345,667 real-world trajectories spanning 80 models, 12 tasks, and 50 harnesses, we extract 318 candidate features that capture both the actions an agent takes at each step (functional) and the language accompanying them (linguistic). We retain only features that show instance-level stability, cross-task consistency, and model discriminability. Factor analysis of the remaining 79 features uncovers six stable, model-attributable factors, two functional and four linguistic. For example, Claude-Sonnet-4.6 and Kimi-K3 exhibit the most planfulness, whereas GPT-5.5 and GPT-5.6 are the least energetic. Moreover, we quantify the "knowledge-action gap" in the wild: these factors correlate only weakly with self-reported Big Five scores, even for conceptually matched pairs such as extraversion and energetic (r = 0.07, p = 0.58). Our work offers a new lens for understanding AI personality, with implications for users, developers, and researchers from both computer science and social science.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint; working in progress</p></details> |
| **[AgentMonopoly: Behavioral Patterns and Persona Control in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29330v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How do LLM agents' strategies differ, and how much can instructions to adopt a persona change their behavior over a game? We study these questions with AgentMonopoly, an environment where agents communicate, buy and trade properties, and make decisions that affect later turns. We compare models from four families, each at two sizes, in games with identical models, different sizes of the same family, or different families. We also assign one agent a persona, a description of how it should behave, intended to change a tendency observed in games without that persona. The tested models differ in how often they trade, waive rent, and manage cash and properties. Larger models do not consistently win more games. Persona instructions change the words agents use and some of their actions, but the changes do not always match the role across all decisions. These results show why persona following should be assessed through actions and outcomes as well as language. As our findings are conditional on the tested checkpoints, prompts, and five seeds per configuration, the open-source nature of AgentMonopoly provides the experimental infrastructure for further extending these comparisons. Our code is available at https://github.com/SuELS-Group/Agent_Monopoly.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 8 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[SARIA: Persistence-Aware KV Caching with Coordinated DRAM--SSD Reload for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31169v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn LLM agents usually reuse long context prefixes, making key value (KV) caching crucial for reducing prefill latency. SSDs provide a low-cost extension to limited DRAM capacity but introduce two key challenges. Eagerly persisting all KV cache blocks incurs substantial write traffic and rapidly consumes finite SSD endurance. Meanwhile, the small number of SSDs provisioned in typical GPU servers limits aggregate reload bandwidth, making SSD-resident KV cache much slower to retrieve than that in DRAM and inflating prefill latency. We present \textsc{Saria} (Selective Admission and Reload for Inference Acceleration), a KV caching system that jointly manages DRAM and SSD by exploiting the session-level temporal locality of LLM agents to reduce SSD writes and accelerate cache reload. On the write path, \textsc{Saria} uses eviction and reuse history to selectively persist KV cache blocks that exhibit reuse opportunities beyond their DRAM residency, avoiding unnecessary writes for KV cache already hosted in DRAM. On the read path, \textsc{Saria} promotes selected SSD-hit KV cache blocks from GPU memory into DRAM after prefill, allowing subsequent reuse within the same session to reduce SSD read traffic while limiting DRAM contention. A transfer coordinator paces DRAM reads while SSD transfers are active, allowing both paths to make progress and efficiently share GPU ingress bandwidth. We implement \textsc{Saria} on LMCache MP and evaluate it using workloads from TraceLab and WEKA, covering both full-attention and hybrid-attention models. The results show that \textsc{Saria} reduces SSD writes by 57.1--58.9\% compared with popular full duplicate caching and the mean time to first token (TTFT) by 42.3--79.7\% over DRAM-only caching.</p></details> | 12 pages, 16 figures |
| **[ARES: Adaptive Reasoning-Effort Steering for PPA- and Cost-Aware RTL Optimization with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.27879v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents optimize the power, performance, and area (PPA) of register-transfer-level (RTL) designs by iterating over edits, synthesis, and PPA analysis, paying a dollar cost for every LLM call. Prior agents report the quality reached without its normalized cost, attribute that quality to an engineered cross-design memory, and hold the reasoning effort of every call fixed. We propose Ares with three corresponding innovations. (1) We introduce a normalized dollar cost per LLM call reported alongside the figure of merit (FoM), enabling fair comparison across effort levels and optimizers. (2) Using this accounting, we find the construction of the long-term memory matters little. An engineered memory brings no dependable gain over a plain concatenation of the same experience. (3) We instead adapt the per-call reasoning effort by escalating to deeper reasoning only once progress at a lower effort stalls, via a patience counter fit on 21 training designs, allocating reasoning where it pays rather than uniformly across all iterations. On three test designs unseen during training, the effort policy lowers the FoM by 22-28% with Opus 4.6 and by 18-34% with GPT-5.5, where the best fixed effort reaches 20-24% and 19-29%, respectively, at equal normalized cost. Ares closes on average 91% of the gap from an LLM-drafted multiply-accumulate unit to its highly hand-optimized counterpart. It also reaches a 5% lower FoM than state-of-the-art Dr. RTL at only 35% of its cost.</p></details> | 8 pages, 6 figures |
| **[SCOPE: Compiling Natural-Language Policies into Neurosymbolic Predicate Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31062v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Policy enforcement for LLM agents has shifted from single-turn content moderation to compound, application-specific policies evaluated over multi-turn transcripts and tool-call trajectories. Fine-tuned guardrail models are efficient but degrade on compound policies, and while LLM-as-a-judge approaches achieve higher accuracy, they incur high inference cost and tend to produce unstable predictions. Neurosymbolic methods offer a natural alternative, decomposing the policy into narrow neural decisions composed under explicit logic. However, existing symbolic layers lack the required expressiveness and are optimized against fixed data rather than the policy itself. To address this, we introduce SCOPE, which compiles a policy into an executable program of neural predicates executed by small models, deterministic extractors, and code that composes their outputs. The program is optimized jointly with its training data, using adversarial generation of counterexamples that target where the program deviates from the policy. We evaluate SCOPE on a benchmark of real-world policy tasks against prompt optimization, task decomposition, and workflow generation methods, achieving the highest average accuracy across tasks and models. Programs optimized with the weakest executor transfer to other models without further optimization. This allows them to be deployed in production on inexpensive models, where they reach accuracy comparable to frontier reasoning models. Unlike these monolithic models, SCOPE produces substantially more stable predictions, and each verdict is interpretable through the predicate outputs that produced it. Additionally, we validate the key components of our method through an ablation study. SCOPE thus enables reliable, interpretable, and efficient policy enforcement at production scale.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agri-SCHOLAR: Simulation-Grounded In-Season Rolling Advisories for Agricultural Crops Using LLM-Bounded Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31003v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing agricultural advisories to farmers suffer from several limitations: (a) remain largely expert-driven, (b) do not adequately account for in-season weather, and (c) do not scale to individual farms. In this paper, our objective is to close these gaps by developing Agri-SCHOLAR (Simulation-Grounded Crop Harvest Optimization with LLM-Guided In-Season Advisory Rollouts). Agri-SCHOLAR is a rolling, in-season advisory framework that issues, at intervals of every few days or every week, a personalized advisory using the following information: soil and crop properties, observed weather to date, short-range forecast of weather, and a season extrapolation of weather based on historical analogs. The novelty and depth of Agri-SCHOLAR lies in each advisory being produced by a closed-loop system which couples multiple LLM agents with a powerful crop simulator (such as APSIM in our case). The simulator crucially serves as an evaluation oracle. In Agri-SCHOLAR, the LLM agents propose agronomically sound and feasible bounds on actionable parameters and an optimizer searches within those bounds against a multi-objective reward spanning yield, resource utilization, and economic feasibility. We conduct a detailed 11-year analysis of the maize crop in two selected locations of South Asia and show that Agri-SCHOLAR consistently outperforms the region's standard packages of practice and satisfactorily addresses the limitations of existing agricultural advisories.</p></details> |  |
| **[ReflectPO: Reflection-Guided Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30933v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>With external tools, large language models (LLMs) can tackle complex tasks through sustained environmental interaction. LLM-based agents are therefore increasingly used to tackle complex long-horizon tasks in real-world environments. However, these tasks involve sequential, interdependent decisions and changes in environmental states. Early execution errors affect subsequent interaction histories and environmental states, and their effects accumulate along the decision chain. To address this challenge, we propose Reflection-Guided Policy Optimization (ReflectPO), a reinforcement learning framework for autonomous error recovery in long-horizon tasks. ReflectPO comprises two core components. The first is Reflection-Guided Sampling. We use branch differences and repeated visits in interaction trajectories to construct hints for error recovery. The second is Tree-Structured Credit Assignment. We organize sampled trajectories into trees and assign action-level credit using parent--child reward differences and recovery gains. Experiments over two long-horizon tool-use benchmarks show that ReflectPO outperforms multiple existing baselines in most domains. It also achieves the best overall performance on average. Further analyses show that ReflectPO effectively improves agents' ability to recognize their own deviations, recover from erroneous states, and continue making progress toward task completion.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Price of Safety: Benign-Case Utility and Token Overhead of Memory-Poisoning Defenses in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22818v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Memory-poisoning defenses for LLM agents are typically evaluated by how well they stop attacks, even though almost all traffic they encounter is benign. We measure their cost when no attack is present. Our harness holds the memory backend, retrieval, answer prompt, and judge constant, changing only the defense. We test four defenses on half of LoCoMo: five long conversations with 750 turns and 255 questions per run, repeated three times each. Replication proved necessary: one run showed a reranker cost on adversarial questions that the other two did not. The three write-time defenses (sanitization, provenance checking, and LLM-based anomaly detection) never fired on benign traffic. We detect no accuracy cost from them at a resolution of about $\pm$4 points; anomaly detection adds 1.2% token overhead. The read-time reranker, with 2.7% token overhead, fired on 9.9% of its memory decisions and withheld memory from 45% of questions. It lowered core accuracy by 4.4 points (95% CI [-9.0, -0.05]; McNemar p = 0.064). The effect is borderline in magnitude, but its direction held across all six runs that used the reranker. A blind audit found content-based justification for only 2 of 50 sampled drops. Stacking all four defenses raised token overhead to 4.1% but did not compound the accuracy cost. In our runs, cost tracked what each defense judged and how much context it used, rather than whether it used an LLM. Attack success rate reveals none of this, so benign cost should be reported alongside it. We release the harness, labels, and per-run outputs.</p></details> |  |

| **[Gandalf: Flash Shall Not Die! We illustrate the framework using 2,957 recorded executions in synthetic compliance-triage and data-operations tasks. Conversely, some compliance cases retained the same tool sequence while recorded decisions changed. A case-level example shows how repeated labels accompanied different lookups and escalation calls. These observations demonstrate how the comparison level changes the description of repeated behavior. The resulting protocol connects configuration control, preservation of each attempt, comparison of records, and inspection of changed cases. | **[Feedback That Backfires in Games: Do LLM Players Repeat the Move They Just Watched Fail?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30205v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When an LLM agent's move fails, the harness writes the move and the failure message into the transcript and asks again. On fixed probe items built by perturbing a correct move, the record backfires: pooled over 8 instruction-tuned models (135M-1.7B) it makes the failed move more likely (corrective gain G = -1.38 nats per token), through the move's surface form, for illegal moves but not for losing ones, and not for every model. An exploratory analysis shows what sets the sign: after the record, the failed move's log-probability barely depends on how likely it was before (slope 0.18). Moves the model would not have played become more likely, and greedy decoding starts to produce them; moves it was about to play become less likely, but greedy decoding still repeats 38% of them. A description of the failure that omits the move string keeps the model's preference instead. Along the OLMo 2 1B post-training ladder the inversion is present in the base model and largely removed at the DPO stage. RAGEN's default models invert in RAGEN's own Sokoban format before any RL. A "do not repeat" instruction does nothing, a clean retry is the worst harness, and a decoder ban stops exact repeats but not illegal play. | **[One Act, Many Masks: Effect-Marginal Policy Optimization for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29427v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in multi-turn reinforcement learning (RL) have substantially improved the performance of LLM agents on complex, long-horizon interactive tasks. Before committing, the agent may query intervention experiments in a laboratory realization of the market. | **[When Agent Evaluation Becomes Unreliable: An Empirical Study of Oracle Failures and Configuration-Dependent Measurement in an LLM Agent Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16067v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent evaluation harnesses are software: they run a test suite, drive a test input generator, and decide outcomes with a test oracle. Overall, by integrating LLM-driven agents, 5uGUE demonstrates significantly better crash discovery performance (12.8x total, 5.2x unique improvement). | **[Deep Reasoning in General Purpose Agents via Structured Meta-Cognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11388v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humans intuitively solve complex problems by flexibly shifting among reasoning modes: they plan, execute, revise intermediate goals, resolve ambiguity through associative judgment, and apply formal procedures to well-specified subproblems. Once that verifier saturates, the loop has nothing left to climb. We instead score agents against the current population, and a frozen panel records whether anyone got better. The panel mean can rise inside the seeds by elimination alone. We therefore count a run as training only when its final mean beats the best generation-zero member. A merge is admitted only when the child moves toward the donor, past the child the same operator produces at zero dose, and the lower confidence bound on that movement reaches half the requested dose. That is the admission test. A detectable shortfall rejects the operator, and the test is otherwise undetermined. We keep realised fitness φas the selector and reference performance θas the unused ruler. Every measurement uses gemini-3.1-flash-lite on one negotiation task, and no fixed-oracle arm was run. In the open loop, tactic append grafts short donor rules and is admitted. Free rewrite moves only a third of the requested dose and is rejected. Template append pastes raw donor replies into the child. Its movement clears the bound on the first probe but not on the re-probe, and it is left undetermined. A held-out re-probe on the five situations the merge never saw confirms that both appends move, and it does not rescue free rewrite. Neither append rises with the dose. Constrained rewrite is admitted in the open loop and was not run in the closed loop, which changes only the operator. The population, the scores and the task stay fixed. No free-rewrite run beats the best generation-zero member. Every tactic-append run and every template-append run does. Sampled loop children keep that split. Free rewrite moves no further toward the donor, and both appends still clear the bound, by less than in the open loop.</p></details> |  |
| **[HybridResearcher: Bridging the Structured-Unstructured Boundary for Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24899v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While LLM agents are increasingly capable of gathering and synthesizing evidence from a single homogeneous source, such as structured tables or unstructured data, real-world analytical questions frequently span heterogeneous sources. To build it, we synthesize cross-source bridge trajectories by starting with validated single-source tasks-such as existing text-to-SQL or web search queries-and fabricating the missing source around a bridge entity drawn from the seed's own output. For seven of the nine principal claims, GRADE gives the lowest certainty rating (Very Low), meaning further studies are likely to produce substantially different figures; each rests on one or two studies in narrow domains. | **[Is Anyone Enforcing the Cartel? Project page: https://douzj-llm.github.io/FALCA/</p></details> |
| **[Local Work, Global Progress: A Convergence Theory of AI Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28286v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Teams of large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly applied to complex tasks, but their collaboration protocols are mostly designed empirically, without convergence guarantees. We formalize collaborative problem solving as block optimization: each agent improves one part of a shared solution, guided by its local objective plus a correction for how its changes affect the other parts. A coordinator checks every proposal against the global objective and merges proposals that do not interfere with each other. We prove linear convergence up to an error floor that vanishes when the corrections are exact. Interfering proposals can also be merged once their combination has been checked; the rate then depends on how well the proposals actually combine, not on worst-case interactions. With GPT-OSS-120B agents on robot routing, a three-agent team finds collision-free plans in every run, better than those of a single agent that controls all robots. On a larger routing task, a six-agent team completes its search about twice as fast as the single agent (in median). Merging the best checked combination of proposals, rather than only proposals that do not interfere, cuts the team's search time by about 30%. With constants measured on the runs, our convergence bound holds in every round and follows the observed progress. We also test the theory on query planning in SQLite and on a synthetic task.</p></details> | <details><summary>59 pa...</summary><p>59 pages, 15 figures, 28 tables. Code: https://github.com/vectozavr/local-work-global-progress</p></details> |
| **[MaD-RSI: Model-as-Data for Recursive Self-Improvement of Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28201v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recursive self-improvement (RSI) of agent harnesses has emerged as an effective approach to enhancing the capabilities of large language model (LLM) agents. By searching the harness space for a specific LLM on a given dataset, RSI can discover a harness tailored to the capabilities and behavioral characteristics of that model. However, such optimization often creates a strong coupling between the learned harness and its target LLM, limiting generalization across models and requiring substantial computational resources to adapt to new LLMs. Moreover, existing RSI methods typically optimize a harness using feedback from a single model and evaluate its generalization to other models only after training. This paradigm treats model differences primarily as a criterion for evaluating generalization rather than as valuable evidence for optimization. In this paper, we introduce a new perspective that treats models as data and places the harness at the center of optimization. Building on this perspective, we propose \textsc{MaD-RSI}, a framework that directly optimizes a shared harness across multiple heterogeneous LLMs by incorporating their diverse failure trajectories into the training process. Rather than assessing model differences only after optimization, \textsc{MaD-RSI} leverages these differences as training evidence to guide the self-improvement of a shared harness. Furthermore, we introduce a new evaluation framework with two complementary metrics designed to assess the effectiveness of harness optimization across models. Experiments across multiple datasets and LLMs demonstrate that \textsc{MaD-RSI} improves generalization over existing approaches while reducing training costs.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgenTest: Mining Business Rules to Test LLM Agents for Business Logic Bugs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28116v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute business operations through tools and interact directly with application data and services. Beyond completing user requests, these agents must obey domain-specific business rules that constrain when actions are permitted and what data or resources may be affected. Violating such rules can lead to business logic bugs, even when a task appears successful. Detecting these bugs is difficult because the rules are rarely available as explicit, testable specifications. They are distributed across system prompts and tool definitions, while others remain implicit in domain knowledge. Existing agent testing approaches typically evaluate task success, predefined policies, or known attack scenarios. This limits their ability to systematically uncover previously unspecified business-rule violations. We present AgenTest, which mines business rules from system prompts, tool definitions, and domain knowledge, refines them into Given-When-Then specifications, and systematically tests each specification. For each extracted specification, AgenTest instantiates its Given conditions by constructing a satisfying environment state and simulated user, exercises the agent through multi-turn interactions, and checks its Then conditions over messages, tool calls, and state changes using a hybrid oracle. On the airline, retail, and telecom agents of tau2-bench with two underlying models, AgenTest produces 551 refined specifications, and detects violations of 89 and 121 distinct specifications, respectively -- 3.4X and 2.5X as many as all baselines combined. Only AgenTest detects cases where an agent improperly accesses or modifies another user's data. Moreover, about 60% of bug-triggering runs cannot be detected from the final state alone, highlighting the need to check the full interaction against explicit business rules.</p></details> |  |
| **[ClawMMU: Protection-Coherent Virtual Memory for Stateful LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28093v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents keep long-term memory, much of which comes from web pages and tool results. Agent harnesses, the software that runs the model, do not record who may act on this memory, so an instruction hidden in a fetched page can be treated as the user's own instruction. We propose ClawMMU, a protection layer that labels every piece of memory with its owner, scope, origins, and authority. ClawMMU checks these labels whenever content enters the model's context, is written to storage, returns from storage, or drives a tool call. Its central rule is that information may rise in authority, but authority never lifts itself. Because the checks depend on where content came from, not on what it says, memory attacks become classical protection faults that the harness stops even when the model is fooled.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the AgenticOS Workshop @ NeurIPS 2026 (non-archival). 6 pages, 1 figure, 1 table</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking How to Remember: Beyond Atomic Facts in Lifelong LLM Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.19952v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To enable reliable long-term interaction, LLM agents require a memory system that can faithfully store, efficiently retrieve, and deeply reason over accumulated dialogue history. Most existing methods adopt an extracted fact based paradigm: handcrafted static prompts compress raw dialogues into atomic facts, which are then stored, matched, and injected into downstream reasoning. Nevertheless, such fact-centric designs inevitably discard fine-grained details in original dialogues and fail to support deep reasoning over scattered isolated facts. To address these limitations, we propose TriMem, which maintains three coexisting representation granularities, including raw dialogue segments anchored by source identifiers for storage fidelity, extracted atomic facts for efficient memory retrieval, synthesized profiles that aggregate dispersed facts into holistic semantic understanding for deep reasoning. We further adopt TextGrad-based prompt optimization, which iteratively refines extraction and profiling prompts via response quality feedback, achieving lifelong evolution without any parameter updating. Extensive experiments on LoCoMo and PerLTQA across multiple LLM backbones demonstrate that TriMem consistently outperforms strong memory baselines. The code is available at https://TMLR-TriMem.github.io .</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Self-Evolving Static Analysis via LLM Pseudo-Labeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30184v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Security bugs often slip past static analysis not because they are impossible to detect, but because detection rules have not yet been written for the code in front of us. The agent receives feedback only from a second LLM critic, which inspects flagged and unflagged code to identify likely false alarms and suspected misses, without access to ground-truth labels. The agent then uses this feedback to revise its queries. On the OWASP Benchmark for Python, SESAA reaches a mean F1 of 0.742 across 8 independent runs, well above the default configurations of five widely used tools and comparable to the strongest of them once it is tuned with access to ground-truth labels that SESAA never sees. In a pilot on real-world Python projects, queries induced from code available before a vulnerability was introduced detect 10 of 19 subsequent CVEs, including 9 detected by rules written for the same repository. The resulting detector is a set of executable, human-readable queries that engineers can inspect, modify, and run independently. These results suggest that LLM agents can turn static-analysis rule maintenance from a manually authored process into an iterative process of automated discovery and repair.</p></details> |  |
| **[QwenGyre: An Elastic Reinforcement Learning Framework for Training xLong-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33848v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly undertake extreme-long (xlong) horizon tasks, where a single execution can span hours, hundreds of model--environment interactions, and nearly 1M tokens per rollout. To address these, we presents QwenGyre, an end-to-end framework for xlong-horizon online RL. QwenGyre elastically reallocates GPUs between rollout and training without interrupting live executions, while its trajectory processor reconstructs branching histories, scores partial progress, and deduplicates redundant paths to bound training costs. Scaled to our flagship model, Qwen~3.8 2.4T, with 700K tokens per rollout, QwenGyre yields a 6.0% absolute gain on NL2RepoBench (52.5% $\to$ 58.5%) in 48 steps. Across our evaluations on diverse domains of training datasets, QwenGyre delivers up to $1.85\times$ and $1.78\times$ speedups over Colocate and Async, respectively.</p></details> |  |
| **[SAPD: Learning from Agent Experience without Environment Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27847v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scaling the training of Large Language Model (LLM) agents typically requires a large collection of executable environments, whose construction, maintenance, and operation constrain the expansion of training experience. To address this challenge, we propose Step-Advantage Policy Distillation (SAPD), a method for learning from offline trajectories using relative step advantages. SAPD decomposes multi-turn trajectories into individual decision contexts for on-policy distillation, allowing the student to generate responses conditioned on recorded histories, and introduces relative step advantage signals to stabilize training. | **[Toward Agentic AI for the 6G Physical Layer: From Algorithm Discovery to Verified Baseband Silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27797v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>6G will be designed faster than it can be built: AI-driven search already proposes physical-layer algorithms at machine speed, while carrying a single one onto embedded baseband silicon remains specialist work. We present an agentic workflow in which an LLM coding agent implements algorithms on a commercial baseband SoC, optimizes them down to agent-written vector assembly, and validates them on silicon. We showcase the workflow on an AI-discovered orthogonal time frequency space (OTFS) detector portfolio on the open 6G4All platform. It yields twelve kernels that run cycle-exactly on silicon, their optimized versions at 18k-405k cycles (30-660 $μ$s) per frame. It also yields a new detector shaped by the chip's measured costs that needs 13.9% fewer cycles than the port of the discovered detector while matching its error rate, with a measured edge our evaluation cannot statistically resolve, a structure that a hardware-blind search objective had no reason to find. What is not specific to OTFS is the workflow: an LLM agent exploring an implementation space under real hardware limits, with the cost of every choice measured rather than assumed.</p></details> | <details><summary>7 pag...</summary><p>7 pages, 3 figures, 5 tables. Accepted at the 2026 IEEE Globecom Workshops (GC Wkshps), 2nd Workshop on Agentic AI Empowered Wireless Communications and Networking, Macau, China, December 2026. (c) 2026 IEEE</p></details> |
| **[GRADUS: Discovering and Recovering Agent Lessons from the Environment's Latent Syllabus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27781v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training LLM agents for policy-constrained tool environments, such as an airline backend with booking, refund, and cancellation policies, requires tasks that exercise the environment's rules individually and in combination. Without seed tasks, Gradus treats a fixed environment as a latent syllabus, extracting guarded behavioral operators that capture how actions depend on current conditions and how their effects reshape subsequent decisions. It composes these operators into increasingly demanding tasks while using corpus-level feedback to target missing outcome combinations. Crucially, Gradus jointly calibrates tasks and solutions against the environment: execution feedback identifies which side to refine while preserving the intended behavioral requirements. Verification thus becomes a recovery signal rather than a terminal filter, coupling coverage-guided exploration with the preservation of difficult examples. From off-the-shelf backbones, the resulting curriculum lifts a 30B model from 59% to 89% on $τ^2$-Bench and from 47% to 83% on CAR-Bench with an Opus 4.5 proposer, and to 76% and 62%, respectively, with an open-weight Qwen3-235B proposer. | **[RITE: Evaluating End-to-End Resilience to Silent Tool Errors in E-Commerce Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27777v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using LLM agents increasingly answer e-commerce queries through multi-step tool interactions. The expected output of a tool call can shift between agent development and execution, allowing a plausible but incorrect value to be returned without an explicit failure signal. To study agents' resilience across this end-to-end process, we formulate Silent-error Resilience Task, an interactive task that evaluates whether they complete the original query after a silent error corrupts an intermediate state. To enable controlled and reproducible evaluation, we use a semantic error taxonomy to inject errors into fixed workflow prefixes, freeze recorded tool behavior, and let agents continue freely. It contains 167 matched units across nine e-commerce workflows, yielding 501 silent-error, correct-value, and explicit-failure instances. Across 10 recent LLMs, silent errors roughly halve task success and are consistently harder than explicit failures. | **[SAFE-Mem: Secure Agentic Memory with Fine-Grained Authority and Governance-Aware Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22959v2)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on persistent memory to support long-term reasoning and personalized interactions. However, information collected from external sources can introduce security risks when untrusted claims (i.e., atomic statements) are later treated as authorized preferences, policies, or permissions. To address these limitations, we propose SAFE-Mem, a fine-grained memory governance framework that improves security while preserving legitimate memory use. First, SAFE-Mem annotates each claim with three governance labels (i.e., kind, authority, and scope) to capture its provenance and permitted use. Second, governance-aware retrieval matches query requirements with claim permissions to block unauthorized influence while retaining legitimate information. Third, a governance-enabled memory graph connects source sentences, claims, and entities to support provenance tracking and efficient retrieval. Across four LLM backbones on MPBench and LoCoMo, SAFE-Mem attains the lowest attack success among defenses that retain legitimate memory, at least 77% below the strongest of them, and the highest answer accuracy of all evaluated defenses, improving it by up to 15 points. The code is available at https://github.com/LVenum/SAFE-Mem.</p></details> |  |
| **[Memory as Code: Declarative Retrieval over Codified Long-Term Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27700v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In long-term memory systems, memories are constructed from interaction histories and retrieved in response to queries, enhancing the reasoning ability of LLM agents. In complex scenarios, facts are scattered across sessions and their relationships evolve over time, making it difficult for existing memory systems to compose them to adapt to diverse queries. Specifically, codified construction builds a three-layer executable memory structure that progresses from an atomic function layer, through a relational layer, to a modular composition layer. | **[Is AI Widening the Wage Gap? A Hybrid Agentic Simulation for Labor Equity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33367v3)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence (AI) is reshaping labor markets, yet its effects on wage distribution and the underlying mechanisms remain insufficiently understood. Conventional analytical approaches are limited in their ability to directly examine the dynamic evolution of worker behavior and income distribution under sustained AI shocks and counterfactual policy scenarios. To address this limitation, we present a hybrid agentic framework that aims to challenges of scalability of rule-based models and the limited explainability in LLM-agentic frameworks. Using this framework and sociodemographic data from China, we simulate changes in wage distribution under repeated AI shocks. The results show that both the average-wage ratio between workers in the top and bottom income deciles (T10/B10) and the Gini coefficient increase persistently, suggesting that AI shocks widen the wage gap and exacerbate income inequality. This pattern of a widening wage gap remains robust across alternative large language model decision engines and 30 Monte Carlo simulations. We further conduct counterfactual policy experiments. The results show that education subsidies targeted at low-income workers increase both the number of skill-upgrading attempts and the number of successful upgrades, with particularly pronounced improvements in the upskilling success probability of workers in the bottom income decile. | **[Local Allow, Global Deny: Why Per-Action Authorization Does Not Compose in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27620v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autonomous LLM agents act through tools under per-action authorization: a local guard screens each tool call in isolation. We show that local authorization does not compose into global authorization. A compositional authorization failure (CAF) is a locally authorized execution, or a pair of locally authorized executions, that violates a global policy spanning intent, purpose, provenance, consent, and delegation. We characterize CAFs along two axes, expressiveness (single-trace safety versus relational k-safety) and observability (a ladder of monitor profiles). Our central result is an impossibility: relational purpose violations form a 2-safety hyperproperty with no per-trace locus of blame, so a per-action monitor that never raises a false alarm detects none of them, independent of the underlying model or decision procedure; they become visible only under cross-execution monitoring, which none of the six guardrails we survey implements. We make this constructive with AL-SYNTH, a SAT/SMT synthesizer (using no language model) that emits minimal CAF witnesses, each checked against an exhaustive oracle. The empirical signature is sharp: across nine LLM judges the per-action view approves the synthesized witness almost without exception, blocking it at a rate comparable to a benign control, yet the same models flag it when shown the two executions together. Per-action authorization is therefore structurally insufficient for relational policy conjuncts not reducible to single-trace safety: to bound them, a monitor must compare executions or accept false alarms.</p></details> | <details><summary>Exten...</summary><p>Extended version of a paper accepted at the 8th IEEE International Conference on Trust, Privacy and Security in Intelligent Systems and Applications (IEEE TPS 2026), with full proofs and appendices. 12 pages, 5 figures, 7 tables. Code and data: https://github.com/anmolpandey299/al-synth</p></details> |

## Medical Large Language Models
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[PFedQFT: Personalized Federated Quantum-Inspired Fine-Tuning for Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25712v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical data remain fragmented across institutional silos because of privacy, governance, and ownership constraints. Federated learning enables medical institutions to adapt large language models (LLMs) collaboratively without transferring raw records. However, independently parameterized low-rank adapters incur repeated parameter and communication costs across Transformer projections, while uniform aggregation may limit adaptation to heterogeneous institutional data. We propose PFedQFT, a quantum-inspired framework for personalized federated fine-tuning with a compact shared adapter. Specifically, PFedQFT constructs reusable orthonormal adaptation bases from compact skew-symmetric generators using a truncated matrix-exponential approximation followed by QR orthonormalization. Compatible projections reuse these bases, while a globally shared full core preserves the flexibility of each projection. Each client further maintains a lightweight private residual operator, whose directions are learned globally and whose coefficients remain local. Experiments on seven public medical tasks and multiple LLM backbones show substantial reductions in trainable parameters and per-round adapter upload volume, with competitive predictive performance on most tasks.</p></details> | 10 pages, 5 figures |
| **[TriageRA-CCF: Source-Side Clinical Confidence and Coverage Signals for Adaptive Rank Budgeting in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29375v2)** | 2026-09-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models are commonly adapted with a fixed low-rank budget, even though medical questions differ substantially in confidence, clinical coverage, and cross-domain difficulty. We study adaptive rank budgeting for parameter-efficient medical question answering: for each question, the adapter decides whether to activate a small, medium, or large subset of LoRA rank channels. The central challenge is that a naive adaptive budget router can collapse to unstable choices or spend capacity without improving shifted benchmarks. We propose TriageRA-CCF, a source-side teacher for adaptive rank-budgeted LoRA. It combines three signals computed only from source training data: base-model answer confidence, metadata-cell clinical coverage, and a counterfactual close-miss proxy. These signals supervise a straight-through budget router over active ranks {2,4,8}, together with budget-cost, entropy, and rank-balance regularization. Under a matched CMB-source training protocol, TriageRA-CCF achieves the best average accuracy among LoRA, DoRA, and MoELoRA baselines on both Qwen3-8B and Llama3.1-8B. The gains are modest and non-uniform across benchmarks: +0.21 average points over the strongest external baseline on Qwen3-8B and +0.16 on Llama3.1-8B. Component ablations show that confidence, coverage, and counterfactual signals all provide useful budget supervision, but their combination is not monotonically best on every backbone.</p></details> |  |
| **[MedRouter: Demystifying Knowledge Differences Across Medical LLMs for Routing-Based Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33119v1)** | 2026-09-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical question answering spans diverse specialties and modalities, and individual medical large language models (LLMs) exhibit distinct strengths across tasks and domains. In this paper, we introduce MedRouter, an agentic system that uses an embedding-based multi-label router to select and query specialist LLMs, then passes their responses to a generator to produce the final answer. We further propose SCALE (Specialist Competence-Aware Learning), a two-stage training framework that first trains the Router with specialist correctness supervision and then optimizes its selections through reinforcement learning. The second stage uses a Performance Gain Reward (PGR) that measures how specialist information affects the generator's answer correctness relative to answering without that information. Experiments on eight text-based and multimodal medical QA benchmarks show that MedRouter outperforms the strongest routing baseline by 8% in average accuracy. Our analysis of specialist outputs further reveals distinct strengths and complementary question-level coverage, motivating learned routing to combine these capabilities for more comprehensive medical reasoning.</p></details> |  |
| **[HiMed: Incentivizing Hindi Reasoning in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.24635v2)** | 2026-09-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models hold promise for reducing healthcare disparities, yet Hindi remains severely underrepresented. | **[A General Multimodal Probing Framework for Data Fusion and Prediction with Frozen Large Language Models: Evidence from Multimodal EHR Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.28798v2)** | 2026-09-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The main objective of this paper is to propose a general framework for prediction based on different sources of multimodal data in the healthcare domain. Multimodal probing of frozen LLM representations provides a practical, backbone-flexible approach for studying EHR modalities and adapting clinical representations across datasets.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages (14 main text + 16 supplementary), 3 figures, 4 tables; supplementary materials appended after the references</p></details> |
| **[The widening evaluation gap in medical large language model research 2023 to 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11770v1)** | 2026-09-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models are superseded every few quarters; clinical evidence takes years. We asked whether medical research is keeping pace with the systems it evaluates. PubMed returned 11,628 records for January 2023 to June 2026 across fourteen clinical domains, growing 45-fold; 2.5% used a randomised, controlled or prospective design. Evaluation lag, from a study's newest named model release to its own publication, widened from 1.33 to 6.08 quarters. Because discontinued models age mechanically, we benchmarked this against a counterfactual holding model composition fixed: migration to newer systems offset only 56% of the drift (95% CI 50-65). Randomised trials evaluated models a median 4.6 quarters older than other designs (P = 3 x 10^-19), yet among studies naming a model still under development no design differed from any other; 62% of randomised trials evaluated a discontinued family. Rigour and currency are in tension, and that tension reflects model selection rather than research timelines.</p></details> |  |
| **[VeriSim: A Configurable Framework for Stress-Testing Medical AI Under Patient Communication Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.10441v2)** | 2026-09-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical large language models are typically evaluated on idealized patient cases that do not reflect how real patients communicate. We introduce VeriSim, a patient simulation framework that injects controllable noise along six clinically grounded communication dimensions while substantially preserving each patient's medical record. Truth adherence is supported by a verifier that extracts atomic claims from each candidate utterance and judges them against a UMLS-grounded vector index built with BioLORD embeddings, using the retrieved atoms' structured clinical metadata (e.g., drug class, anatomical site, treats-condition relations) rather than surface-text similarity alone. Across seven open-weight LLMs, realistic noise reduces diagnostic accuracy by 15-25 percentage points and increases conversation length by 34-55%; the 7-8B models degrade 1.4x more than 70B+ models. A board-certified physician and a licensed nurse rate VeriSim's conversations highly on truth, realism, clinical utility, and noise fidelity (inter-annotator agreement >= 0.80 across all dimensions), and an LLM-as-judge closely tracks their ratings, enabling scalable evaluation. We developed a multi-domain red teaming framework evaluating eleven contemporary LLMs across 690 clinically grounded scenarios spanning nine domains and over 150 subcategories. Scenarios incorporated adversarial transformations, and responses were assessed using a seven-dimension rubric with LLM-assisted scoring and human-in-the-loop validation. Results revealed substantial performance variance, with mean scores ranging from 0.791 to 0.984. Critically, several high-performing systems produced complete failures in individual safety-critical scenarios, demonstrating that aggregate accuracy masks clinically meaningful risk. The highest-performing systems (X-BAI, GPT-5, Claude Opus 4.1) achieved scores above 0.97 with low variance, while performance varied significantly across domains. Equity-related tasks showed 10-20% error amplification with demographic modifications, and human reviewers identified clinically relevant failures missed by automated evaluation. Our findings demonstrate that performance variance and worst-case failures provide more clinically meaningful reliability indicators than mean accuracy alone, and that hybrid evaluation approaches combining automation with clinician oversight are essential for credible safety assessment.</p></details> | <details><summary>10 pa...</summary><p>10 pages, 4 figures. To be presented at the Text2Story 2026 Workshop (Delft, The Netherlands, 29 March 2026); CEUR Workshop Proceedings (forthcoming). Affiliation: John Snow Labs Inc</p></details> |
| **[From Retinal Evidence to Safe Decisions: RETINA-SAFE and ECRT for Hallucination Risk Triage in Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.05348v1)** | 2026-04-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hallucinations in medical large language models (LLMs) remain a safety-critical issue, particularly when available evidence is insufficient or conflicting. We study this problem in diabetic retinopathy (DR) decision settings and introduce RETINA-SAFE, an evidence-grounded benchmark aligned with retinal grading records, comprising 12,522 samples. RETINA-SAFE is organized into three evidence-relation tasks: E-Align (evidence-consistent), E-Conflict (evidence-conflicting), and E-Gap (evidence-insufficient). We further propose ECRT (Evidence-Conditioned Risk Triage), a two-stage white-box detection framework: Stage 1 performs Safe/Unsafe risk triage, and Stage 2 refines unsafe cases into contradiction-driven versus evidence-gap risks. ECRT leverages internal representation and logit shifts under CTX/NOCTX conditions, with class-balanced training for robust learning. Under evidence-grouped (not patient-disjoint) splits across multiple backbones, ECRT provides strong Stage-1 risk triage and explicit subtype attribution, improves Stage-1 balanced accuracy by +0.15 to +0.19 over external uncertainty and self-consistency baselines and by +0.02 to +0.07 over the strongest adapted supervised baseline, and consistently exceeds a single-stage white-box ablation on Stage-1 balanced accuracy. These findings support white-box internal signals grounded in retinal evidence as a practical route to interpretable medical LLM risk triage.</p></details> |  |
| **[MAPLE: Elevating Medical Reasoning from Statistical Consensus to Process-Led Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08987v1)** | 2026-03-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in medical large language models have explored Test-Time Reinforcement Learning (TTRL) to enhance reasoning. However, standard TTRL often relies on majority voting (MV) as a heuristic supervision signal, which can be unreliable in complex medical scenarios where the most frequent reasoning path is not necessarily the clinically correct one. In this work, we propose a novel and unified training paradigm that integrates medical process reward models with TTRL to bridge the gap between test-time scaling (TTS) and parametric model optimization. Specifically, we advance the TTRL framework by replacing the conventional MV with a fine-grained, expert-aligned supervision paradigm using Med-RPM. This integration ensures that reinforcement learning is guided by medical correctness rather than mere consensus, effectively distilling search-based intelligence into the model's parametric memory. Extensive evaluations on four different benchmarks have demonstrated that our developed method consistently and significantly outperforms current TTRL and standalone PRM selection. Our findings establish that transitioning from stochastic heuristics to structured, step-wise rewards is essential for developing reliable and scalable medical AI systems</p></details> |  |
| **[Silent Sabotage During Fine-Tuning: Few-Shot Rationale Poisoning of Compact Medical LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.02262v1)** | 2026-02-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) is essential for the development of medical large language models (LLMs), yet prior poisoning studies have mainly focused on the detectable backdoor attacks. We propose a novel poisoning attack targeting the reasoning process of medical LLMs during SFT. Unlike backdoor attacks, our method injects poisoned rationales into few-shot training data, leading to stealthy degradation of model performance on targeted medical topics. Results showed that knowledge overwriting was ineffective, while rationale poisoning caused significant decline on the accuracy of the target subject, as long as no correct samples of the same subject appear in the dataset. A minimum number and ratio of poisoned samples was needed to carry out an effective and stealthy attack, which was more efficient and accurate than catastrophic forgetting. We demonstrate though this study the risk of SFT-stage poisoning, hoping to spur more studies of defense in the sensitive medical domain.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeVisE: Behavioral Testing of Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.15339v3)** | 2026-02-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied in clinical decision support, yet current evaluations rarely reveal whether their outputs reflect genuine medical reasoning or superficial correlations. We introduce DeVisE (Demographics and Vital signs Evaluation), a behavioral testing framework that probes fine-grained clinical understanding through controlled counterfactuals. Using intensive care unit (ICU) discharge notes from MIMIC-IV, we construct both raw (real-world) and template-based (synthetic) variants with single-variable perturbations in demographic (age, gender, ethnicity) and vital sign attributes. We evaluate eight LLMs, spanning general-purpose and medical variants, under zero-shot setting. Model behavior is analyzed through (1) input-level sensitivity, capturing how counterfactuals alter perplexity, and (2) downstream reasoning, measuring their effect on predicted ICU length-of-stay and mortality. Overall, our results show that standard task metrics obscure clinically relevant differences in model behavior, with models differing substantially in how consistently and proportionally they adjust predictions to counterfactual perturbations.</p></details> | <details><summary>Camer...</summary><p>Camera-ready version published at Findings of the EACL 2026</p></details> |
| **[PeruMedQA: Benchmarking Large Language Models (LLMs) on Peruvian Medical Exams -- Dataset Construction and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.11517v2)** | 2026-02-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>BACKGROUND: Medical large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance in answering medical examinations.
