We perform extensive experiments and conduct an in-depth analysis from diverse perspectives, which culminate in the following findings: (1) Chinese medical LLMs underperform on this benchmark, highlighting the need for significant advances in clinical knowledge and diagnostic precision. (2) Several general-domain LLMs surprisingly possess considerable medical knowledge. These findings elucidate both the capabilities and limitations of LLMs within the context of MedBench, with the ultimate goal of aiding the medical research community.</p></details> | accepted by AAAI-24 |

## Large Language Models
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Reliable AI-Assisted Decisions via Selective Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31662v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We consider online monitoring of an AI system that produces a sequence of decisions. At each round, the system outputs a proposed decision together with a confidence score estimating the trustworthiness of the decision. The decision and confidence score need not be produced by the same model: for example, one agent may propose a decision, while a separate verifier agent evaluates it and outputs a correctness or safety score. Based on this information, we must decide whether to accept the AI decision automatically or consult an expert, such as a human auditor, who reviews the proposal and either accepts or corrects it. We formulate this task as an online constrained optimization problem in which the goal is to minimize the frequency of expert auditing while guaranteeing that the fraction of AI errors that go unaudited stays below a user-defined threshold. We derive a family of algorithms with provable error-control guarantees with no assumptions, including a method that is optimally efficient: it asymptotically attains the best achievable auditing rate for both adversarial and i.i.d. data streams. Experiments on screening harmful requests to large language models, qualitative text annotation, and data labeling show that our methods achieve valid error control in practice while substantially reducing the need for human auditing compared to baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Does RL Post-training Induce Skill Composition? A Case Study on Countdown](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.01775v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While reinforcement learning (RL) successfully enhances reasoning in large language models, its role in fostering compositional generalization (the ability to synthesize novel skills from known components) is often conflated with mere length generalization. To this end, we study what RL post-training teaches about skill composition and how the composition structure affects the skill transfer. We focus on the Countdown task (given n numbers and a target, form an expression that evaluates to the target) and analyze model solutions as expression trees, where each subtree corresponds to a reusable subtask and thus can be viewed as a "skill." Tracking tree shapes and their success rates over training, we find: (i) out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization to larger n and to unseen tree shapes, indicating compositional reuse of subtasks; (ii) the order of skill acquisition depends upon the composition structure---models master shallow balanced trees (workload is balanced between subtasks) before deep unbalanced ones, with persistent fragility on right-heavy structures for a fixed composition depth. Our diagnostic reveals what is learned, in what order, and where generalization fails, clarifying how RL-only post-training induces OOD generalization beyond what standard metrics such as pass@k reveal. To study the implications of our findings for real-world tasks, we present a preliminary study on formal theorem proving.</p></details> |  |
| **[Cost-Aware Abstention for LLM-Based IT Ticket Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31657v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wrong automatic routing of an IT support ticket can cost more than human triage. We evaluate cost-aware abstention as an offline, cost-conditional readiness assessment: select a routing policy on calibration data under explicit costs, then evaluate that frozen policy once on held-out test data. On a public 47,837-ticket, eight-class corpus, we compare four trained non-LLM classifiers with four large language models under zero- and few-shot prompting. Under the illustrative cost model (c_ok, c_triage, c_wrong) = (0, 1, 5), TF-IDF with logistic regression and a threshold of 0.60 selected on calibration reduces test cost from 0.763 to 0.472, a 38.1% relative reduction (paired ratio-bootstrap 95% CI [35.7%, 40.4%]), while lowering wrong routes from 15.3% to 3.5%. Frozen calibration-selected policies also beat both always-route and always-defer on held-out test data in 14 of 16 settings in a post-hoc cost-parameter analysis. The strongest prompted configuration reaches 0.477 accuracy on a stratified 1,000-ticket test subset, versus 0.879 for fine-tuned DistilBERT on the same tickets. Isotonic lowers expected calibration error without reliably improving discrimination: GPT-4o-mini few-shot has one confidence value before and after recalibration and correctness AUROC 0.500. We therefore report correctness AUROC and tie-aware risk-coverage area alongside ECE. At the analytically derived break-even probability 0.80, seven prompted configurations defer all tickets; Haiku few-shot routes only 19/1,000. An LLM-only agreement control and margin/entropy rejection baselines further separate calibration from discrimination. The findings are scoped to this dataset, prompt design, model endpoints, and illustrative costs; the work is an evaluation framework, not a new abstention algorithm or universal deployment certification.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 38th IEEE International Conference on Tools with Artificial Intelligence (ICTAI 2026). 9 pages, 5 figures, 9 tables</p></details> |
| **[TabAgent: Distilling LLM Agent Components into Cheap Classifiers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16429v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic systems, AI architectures that autonomously execute multi-step workflows to achieve complex goals, are often built using repeated large language model (LLM) calls for closed-set decision tasks such as routing, shortlisting, gating, and verification. While convenient, this design makes deployments slow and expensive due to cumulative latency and token usage. We propose TabAgent, a framework for replacing generative decision components in closed-set selection tasks with a compact textual-tabular classifier trained on execution traces. TabAgent (i) extracts structured schema, state, and dependency features from trajectories (TabSchema), (ii) augments coverage with schema-aligned synthetic supervision (TabSynth), and (iii) scores candidates with a lightweight classifier (TabHead). On the long-horizon AppWorld benchmark, TabAgent maintains task-level success while eliminating shortlist-time LLM calls, reducing latency by approximately 95% and inference cost by 85-91%. Beyond tool shortlisting, TabAgent generalizes to other agentic decision heads, establishing a paradigm for learned discriminative replacements of generative bottlenecks in production agent architectures.</p></details> | <details><summary>67 pa...</summary><p>67 pages, 14 figures, 26 tables. v2 is a substantial revision with a new title and two added authors. The evaluation is extended from AppWorld to seven agent benchmarks across three decision families (set retrieval, classification, policy and reward), and TabHead is now a CatBoost classifier with an ablation over classical and neural heads</p></details> |
| **[SympFormer: Inertial Attention Blocks from Damped Hamiltonian Dynamics on Spaces of Probability Measures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31600v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The attention mechanism underpins modern large language models, yet their dynamics remain only partially understood. Recent perspectives interpret attention blocks as interacting particle systems, whose mean-field limits correspond to gradient flows of energy functionals on spaces of probability densities on $\mathbb R^d$ equipped with Wasserstein-type metrics. We extend this viewpoint by deriving accelerated attention blocks from inertial, Nesterov-type damped Hamiltonian dynamics on these density spaces, developing the geometry and differential calculus needed to make the construction rigorous. In particular, we introduce a local-mobility geometry for the Otto-Wasserstein and softmax-Wasserstein settings, and a quotient Stein geometry that resolves the degeneracy of the linear-attention kernel to yield a well-defined metric and differential calculus. We provide rigorous foundations when softmax attention admits a gradient-flow formulation and when its inertial counterpart admits a damped Hamiltonian on explicitly characterized domains. For linear attention, we obtain a finite-dimensional solution theory with sharp conditions for global existence versus finite-time second-moment blow-up. We further prove a quantitative link between continuous moment blow-up and the discrete-layer depth at which token norms cross a prescribed threshold. Finally, motivated by the finite-token geometry, we propose geometric corrections to token and momentum updates. In decoder-only, nanoGPT-style experiments on TinyStories and OpenWebText, these corrections lower validation negative log-likelihood on next-token prediction relative to momentum-only controls.</p></details> | <details><summary>97 pa...</summary><p>97 pages, 5 figures, 4 tables, comments welcome!</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Naturalness: Probing Automated Text-To-Speech Evaluators on Linguistically Grounded Dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09930v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated Text-to-Speech (TTS) evaluation methods (Mean Opinion Score (MOS) predictors and Audio Large Language Model (Audio-LLM) judges) are expected to reflect human perception, yet it is unclear how well they capture the distinct aspects of speech that listeners actually perceive. We deconstruct "naturalness" into a linguistically grounded annotation schema spanning 10 distinct perceptual dimensions, and use it to construct the first dimension-level meta-evaluation benchmark for TTS, comprising 860 utterances annotated by trained linguist raters. Results from benchmarking four MOS predictors and four Audio-LLM judges reveal that MOS predictors are more sensitive to acoustic signal-level properties at the prosodic and paralinguistic levels, while Audio-LLM judges show selective, prompt-dependent detection that favors word-level errors and does not generalize across all dimensions. Neither class reliably captures a breadth of linguistically structured, human-perceptible speech errors. Our dataset, annotation schema, and evaluation code are publicly released to support more targeted and interpretable TTS evaluation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026 (Main Conference)</p></details> |
| **[VERA: A Responsible AI Dashboard for Assessing LLM Deployments in Light of the EU AI Act](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31588v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Institutions that deploy large language models in regulated settings often need technical evidence of responsible AI practices before approving a deployment. COMPL-AI provides a technical interpretation of the EU AI Act that organises this evidence under six principle groups and eighteen technical requirements, twelve of which current benchmarks can measure. The benchmark suites that produce this evidence are typically operated from a command line, while the staff who approve deployments are non-engineers. We present VERA, an open-source self-hosted dashboard deployed to facilitate responsible AI processes within a large European bank. A reviewer selects a model and runs public benchmarks against it. VERA scores the twelve requirements that current benchmarks can measure and instruments the energy consumed by each evaluation, adding run-level evidence for environmental impact. We run four served open-weight models through the requirement pipeline, and the dashboard displays a score for each measurable requirement. A within-subject study then compares how participants read these benchmark results: from the raw output files in one condition, and through the VERA dashboard in the other. The dashboard reduced the time needed to perform the responsible AI assessment, reducing the time required from 56 seconds using raw files to 30 seconds using our dashboard, on average. Our dashboard scored highly (4.36 out of 5) on usefulness in the survey conducted within the organization. The dashboard and the study material are released publicly to promote replicability and reproducibility in different organisational contexts.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 33rd Asia-Pacific Software Engineering Conference (APSEC 2026), Software Engineering in Practice track, Bali, Indonesia, 7-10 December 2026. 11 pages, 3 figures</p></details> |
| **[Who Is Sycophantic to Whom? Confident LLMs, Human Deference, and the Collapse of Trust Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31579v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Research on sycophancy asks whether large language models (LLMs) bend towards what their users want to hear. We ask the reverse question: when an LLM is confident, consistent, and wrong, do users bend towards it? In a within-subjects experiment, 32 participants learned up to five science topics by chatting with five assistants built on the same model (GPT-5-mini). Unknown to participants, each assistant's briefing had 0, 25, 50, 75, or 100% of its facts replaced by plausible falsehoods, and every assistant spoke with full confidence. A surprise quiz after each topic offered the correct fact, the planted falsehood, and two distractors, with a confidence rating for every answer. Across 104 quizzes and 1,040 answers, scores fell from 7.4 to 2.9 out of 10, and the planted option was chosen on 60% of falsified items against 11% of correctly taught ones (odds ratio 12.5). Transcript coding showed that whenever the assistant stated a falsehood, it was adopted about 65% of the time at every falsification level. Absorbed falsehoods were held with high confidence (8.4/10), the modeled confidence advantage of right over wrong answers vanished, and the discrimination of confidence for correctness fell from an AUROC of 0.70 to chance, while post-task trust ratings barely moved. Participants tended to return to the chat more when the assistant was worse, but returning went with more absorption, not less, and 30% of all turns pasted quiz questions back to the source being checked. The assistant itself was almost never sycophantic and never conceded under pushback. We introduce a baseline-corrected Human Deference Index to quantify this human-side sycophancy and argue that trustworthiness is a property of the human-AI pair, that sycophancy evaluations should measure both directions, and that learning interfaces must make reliability legible claim by claim.</p></details> | <details><summary>49 pa...</summary><p>49 pages; 32 figures; 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking Sequence Compression with Test-time Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31574v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Sequence compression is a fundamental operation and a critical information bottleneck in efficient language and multimodal models, and yet prevailing approaches based on pooling or learned projections remain underperforming. We revisit sequence compression through the lens of test-time regression: input tokens define a regression problem whose fitted coefficients form the compressed representation. Importantly, the regression problem is learned end-to-end via backpropagation, while its coefficients are computed efficiently in closed form during the forward pass. This leads to TTR-Compressor, a general alternative to conventional pooling and projection-based compression. We evaluate TTR-Compressor as the compressor for block-wise sparse attention and the visual token merger for multimodal large language models. On block-wise sparse attention, TTR-Compressor improves RULER performance over attention-based compressors by a large margin. For visual token merging, it consistently outperforms the standard MLP merger and matches its performance with 55.6% fewer visual tokens. These results establish test-time regression as an effective primitive for sequence compression in efficient language and multimodal models.</p></details> | <details><summary>Techn...</summary><p>Technical report (19 pages, 4 figures, project page: https://spherelab.ai/ttr-compressor)</p></details> |
| **[Social-HUMINT: An LLM-Augmented Unified Framework for Measuring Social Media Exposure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31573v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In the post-API landscape, social-media profiling has become inexpensive and automated: Large Language Models (LLMs) can extract structured information from unstructured content and deduce attributes users never disclosed. A person's true exposure is not what they post but what can be inferred from it. As these practices become routine, exposure to social engineering attacks becomes an operational certainty, demanding a shared framework for assessment and mitigation. We make this exposure measurable by quantifying how accurately a target can be profiled, and how far that profile enables social engineering attacks. Because no existing model fully spans this continuum, we unify Social Media Intelligence (SOCMINT) and Cyber Human Intelligence (Cyber-HUMINT), two individual-focused disciplines that evolved separately, into a single doctrine-grounded taxonomy of 38 capabilities across the Cyber Threat Intelligence lifecycle, where user profiling bridges passive collection and active engagement. Domain experts validated the taxonomy (Fleiss' $κ$ = 0.92). We operationalize its core path (extraction, inference, and susceptibility assessment) as an LLM-augmented multi-agent pipeline, evaluated on real participants' public data. Susceptibility is assessed indirectly: the pipeline predicts how each target would rank personalized engagement options, matching their actual choices significantly above chance. This ranking capacity captures the personalization on which tailored attacks depend. The underlying profiles are accurate: extraction reaches F1 = 97.3%, and inferred attributes score 4.73/5, both far above rule-based and NER-based baselines at negligible cost. The framework augments security analysts' tradecraft rather than replacing it and enables defenders to gauge their own exposure before an adversary does.</p></details> |  |
| **[CellTrack-Bench: Microscopic Cell Tracking Benchmark for Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31566v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models have shown outstanding performance on text-first tasks such as coding, math, and writing. Recent efforts are shifting models' training to include scientific problems that often live in highly specialized domains beyond text, such as protein structure and biological images. However, in these scenarios, their general-purpose accuracy compared with specialized algorithms remains in question. One such domain is cell tracking, which requires spatiotemporal awareness of cell dynamics. To evaluate this, we created a new benchmark, CellTrack-Bench, which assesses Vision-Language Models' (VLMs) cell-tracking-related performance against specialized trackers. The full benchmark draws from 15 standard cell-tracking datasets across four imaging modalities and comprises 1,537 problems corresponding to 9,041 cell-linking decisions. We selected a hard subset requiring greater reasoning effort by identifying failures shared across distinct algorithmic trackers. With this proposed dataset, we: (i) evaluated different environment harnesses for VLMs; (ii) benchmarked 9 frontier closed-source models and 13 open-weight models; and (iii) provided a detailed analysis of their failure modes. Code, the stored model outputs, and a pipeline that regenerates the benchmark from the source datasets are available at https://github.com/royerlab/celltrack-bench.</p></details> |  |
| **[Contestable Agents for Data Governance: Recording Human Stewardship of AI-Generated Metadata Across Structured and Unstructured Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31553v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Industrial organizations hold operational knowledge in structured records, such as telemetry, point lists, and transactions, and in unstructured sources, such as manuals, service notes, communications, and inspection reports. Governance programs often manage these sources separately. Classifying, linking, and documenting them depends on a small group of employees with deep domain knowledge. Agents built on large language models can propose metadata, cite documents, and flag quality issues, but unreviewed proposals can spread errors into downstream systems. A perfunctory approval leaves no record of what a reviewer checked. We present contestable governance: specialist agents produce scored, cited arguments for disputed metadata decisions; a steward can accept, reject, modify, or add arguments before commit; and a governance record retains the evidence, interventions, and outcome. A versioned domain glossary supplies model-specific terms and acronym definitions to the agents. We describe the workflow, a triage path that reserves full argumentation for uncertain proposals while preserving review before commit, the scoring and record models, and a gate that controls which verified decisions become precedent. Two HVAC use cases illustrate the pattern: point classification using Brick Schema class labels, supported by an open reference implementation, and an illustrative warranty failure-mode decision linking claim fields and telemetry to service notes and revisioned bulletins.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 2 figures, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[CAMGen: A Core Change-Aware Multi-View Framework for Commit Message Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31552v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Commit messages help developers understand code changes and support software maintenance. Recent approaches use repository context to improve commit message generation (CMG) with large language models (LLMs). However, existing CMG methods provide limited support for understanding how local edits affect functionality jointly implemented by multiple code entities. This functional understanding is essential for generating commit messages that explain the main change and its impact. To address this problem, we propose CAMGen, a core change-aware multi-view commit message generation framework that incorporates functional context into generation. CAMGen identifies core entities that represent the primary change intent. It then forms candidate functional units through repository-wide semantic clustering and uses those containing the core entities to construct a Functional Semantic View. This view supplies semantic context for interpreting the affected functionality. CAMGen also uses context provided by the Structural Dependency View and the Historical Exemplar View. The context from all three views is then provided to the LLM together with the code diff to guide CMG. The experimental results show that CAMGen outperforms representative CMG baselines across three programming languages. Human evaluation further shows that CAMGen generates more complete commit messages with clearer explanations of change rationale and impact. These results demonstrate the value of functional context and complementary repository information for CMG.</p></details> | <details><summary>32 pa...</summary><p>32 pages, 5 figures, 8 tables, Manuscript submitted to a Journal (2026)</p></details> |
| **[MLEvolve: A Self-Evolving Framework for Automated Machine Learning Algorithm Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06473v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly applied to long-horizon tasks such as scientific discovery and machine learning engineering (MLE), where sustained self-evolution becomes a key capability. Evaluation on MLE-Bench shows that MLEvolve achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple dimensions including average medal rate and valid submission rate under a 12-hour budget (half the standard runtime). Moreover, MLEvolve also outperforms specialized algorithm discovery methods including AlphaEvolve on mathematical algorithm optimization tasks, demonstrating strong cross-domain generalization. Existing automated generation methods often suffer from homogeneity, limiting model generalization, and unsolvability, where hallucinated scene graphs create physical mismatches with task goals. This raises a practical question: can a lightweight harness using a single model role achieve competitive performance without additional training? We introduce OPTICYCLE and OPTISTATE, two lightweight inference harnesses for optimization modeling, and evaluate them on nine benchmarks comprising 2,634 problems using 11 Qwen variants spanning 0.6B-27B parameters. Our results show that execution-guided revision consistently improves macro accuracy over direct generation for all evaluated models, with gains of up to 49.46 percentage points. Despite their simplicity, both harnesses outperform six adapted agentic workflows in same-model comparisons and four evaluated post-trained optimization models at comparable model sizes, without additional training. Explicit formulation state provides further but model-dependent improvements, suggesting that additional structure is not universally beneficial.</p></details> |  |
| **[Silicon Samples Match the Answers but Not the Respondents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30900v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Silicon sampling generates survey respondents by having a large language model (LLM) answer a survey as a person with given demographic attributes, and the resulting synthetic populations are increasingly used in place of human ones. None of these compares the respondents themselves, so a generator whose respondents have combinations of answers found in no human respondent can still score well if it reproduces every answer distribution. We propose the respondent coverage gap (RCG), which represents each respondent as a point defined by their complete answer profile and measures how far each human respondent is from the nearest generated one, relative to the distance between human samples. On two US social surveys, all six LLMs we evaluate leave human respondents uncovered, and under our main settings, they fall behind a baseline that samples each answer independently from the human marginals. Across the prompting and inference settings we test, every LLM has a higher RCG than a Gaussian copula fitted to the human data.</p></details> |  |
| **[DGA$_2$D: Directed Graph-Guided Automated Algorithm Design with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00700v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid development of Large Language Models (LLMs) has opened new avenues for Automated Heuristic Design (AHD) for solving NP-hard combinatorial optimization problems (COPs). However, existing LLM-driven AHD methods are largely confined to rigid solver templates, relegating the search process to isolated module tuning. Transitioning to fully autonomous, system-level algorithm design is essential but fraught with low reliability of generated operators, extremely large search spaces, and ineffective credit assignment. To overcome these drawbacks, this paper proposes a Directed Graph-Guided Automated Algorithm Design framework, termed DGA$_2$D. It structures the open-ended program space as a directed graph, where each node represents a functional operator that can be instantiated using one of multiple candidate code implementations, while directed walks constitute complete algorithmic pipelines. A first-order path-dependent credit assignment mechanism is introduced to evaluate code variations strictly based on their topological context. Extensive experiments across 12 distinct COPs, ranging from complex scheduling to routing, demonstrate the consistent empirical advantages of DGA$_2$D. It reduces the average normalized gap by up to 10.96 percentage points compared to state-of-the-art LLM baselines.</p></details> | <details><summary>46 pa...</summary><p>46 pages, 5 figures, including supplementary material</p></details> |
| **[Building Presentation Intelligence for High-Quality Slide Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30888v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>PowerPoint presentations (PPTs) are a dominant medium for communication across diverse domains, yet high-quality PPT generation still depends heavily on labor-intensive human effort. In this work, we present a unified training framework for automatic PPT generation toward presentation intelligence, integrating cross-validated data synthesis, guided teacher enhancement, and verifiable reward optimization. We first construct a cross-validated pipeline for data synthesis, with explicit control over both difficulty and validity. We then develop a teacher-enhancement framework grounded in rules and prior knowledge to substantially strengthen the instructional capacity of teacher models. Building on this, we further improve supervised fine-tuning (SFT) through a mixed-teacher strategy and a lightweight outcome-driven trajectory filtering method. Finally, based on the resulting SFT models, we introduce a suite of verifiable aesthetic metrics together with a GRPO-based reinforcement learning framework to optimize slide-level visual attributes beyond text-only supervision. Experiments show that our enhanced SFT pipeline improves Qwen-3.6-27B and Qwen-3.6-35B-A3B by 7.45 and 9.35 points on PresentBench, respectively, surpassing GLM-5.2 while maintaining strong generalization. Reinforcement learning further improves performance across five aesthetic dimensions, compensating for the limitations of SFT in visual refinement. Overall, the results demonstrate the effectiveness of our framework for high-quality PPT generation.</p></details> | 28 pages |
| **[Tracing Subliminal Learning Through Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30884v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models trained on data generated by other models can sometimes inherit traits unrelated to that data, a phenomenon called subliminal learning. We study both hard label distillation, where students train on numbers generated by a biased teacher, and on-policy soft label distillation, where students generate the data and the teacher's logits provide the learning signal. Using Pythia, PolyPythia, and DataDecide OLMo models, we find that transfer persists in both settings across differences in weight initialization, pretraining data order, pretraining corpus, model size, and architecture. Across pretraining checkpoints, the ability to transmit a preference and the ability to acquire one develop separately: some checkpoints are more effective teachers but weaker students, and vice versa. Consistent with this distinction, cross-model transfer can be strongly asymmetric in magnitude when teacher and student roles are reversed. To investigate what carries the preference signal, we construct synthetic number sequences that match the teacher's generated number frequencies but have no dependence on the prompt or preceding numbers and find that these still induce substantial transfer in the hard-label setting. In top-$K$ soft-label distillation, removing animal tokens from the loss reduces transfer, although not completely in the same-model setting. In our experiments, soft label distillation produces more consistent transfer with less extensive training than hard label distillation. The growing use of on-policy distillation in model post-training makes this a particularly relevant setting for assessing unintended preference transfer.</p></details> |  |
| **[Group Contrastive Policy Optimization: Learning Beyond the Self-Exploration Limit with Golden Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.07790v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has become a powerful paradigm for improving the reasoning abilities of large language models, yet group-based methods lose their learning signal when every sampled response receives zero reward. We propose Group Contrastive Policy Optimization (GCPO), a hybrid objective that preserves standard on-policy optimization for informative rollout groups and activates a proximal contrastive objective only when exploration completely fails. The auxiliary objective pairs an offline golden reasoning trajectory with the current policy's failed responses, thereby providing a reliable positive reference and prompt-specific hard negatives without treating the golden trajectory as an on-policy rollout. Its old-policy token probabilities are evaluated through teacher forcing and used solely to constrain the update, rather than as an importance-sampling correction for the teacher distribution. A first-order analysis shows that the resulting update increases a length-normalized likelihood margin between reliable reasoning and observed failures. | **[TRIAD: Benchmarking Omni-Modal Ambiguity in Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30841v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Ambiguity is a central feature of natural communication: a sentence, image, or sound may remain underspecified until evidence from the other channels is considered. Yet current multimodal benchmarks mostly test perception, recognition, or reasoning over already determinate inputs, leaving unclear whether omni-modal large language models can resolve ambiguity distributed across text, vision, and audio. We introduce TRIAD, a benchmark for omni-modal ambiguity resolution. Each item is a text--image--audio triplet paired with a question and a set of answer options, and is constructed so that the full triplet determines a unique answer while removing any one modality makes the set of consistent options non-unique. TRIAD contains $327$ hand-written queries in $86$ scene groups and covers $18$ ambiguity categories across the three modalities. Evaluating $14$ omni-modal systems reveals that strong item-level performance does not translate into robust scene-level disambiguation: the best model still trails humans by over $20$ points in group accuracy. Removing answer options widens the gap further, driving model group accuracy to the single digits while humans remain robust. Leave-one-out and transcript/description counterfactuals further show that current systems often underuse audio cues when text and image are present. | **[Structure then Query: Enabling Precise Analytical Queries over Unstructured Documents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.13384v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Unstructured documents constitute the majority of enterprise and web data. However, because mainstream retrieval methods still relies on fuzzy matching based on vector similarity, accurately obtaining information and performing structured analysis and reasoning remains a major challenge. To address these limitations, AnnoIndex introduces two core fundamental components. The first is Annotation Index. The system uses a module called SchemaLoop to automatically create hierarchical annotation schemas from the raw corpus, and then uses lightweight language model to extract specific values. It turns scattered unstructured text into a materialized, structured index that enables low-cost filtering and querying. The annotation index avoids the black-box matching of vector similarity and amortizes attribute extraction costs from online queries to a one-time build. The second innovation is a Structured Query Engine. It compiles user questions into execution plans based on SQL extension. It first uses the Annotation Index for precise documents filtering, then gradually applies extraction operations in ascending order of cost, resorting to LLMs only for the remaining minimal fraction of the corpus that require deep semantic understanding. The extracted attributions are merged into the annotation index, reducing the cost of future queries. | **[ChatT2: An Adaptive Framework for Developing a Large Language Model-Based Agent for Natural Product Domain Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25620v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific investigations into microbial natural products (NPs) present significant challenges for novices, largely due to the complexity of microbial systems, biochemical diversity, technical skill requirements, and the demands of bioinformatics and data analysis processes. Our research highlights how ChatT2, designed with this multiagent framework, addresses the challenges faced by general LLMs in terms of understanding limited, specialized corpora and complex biological information and provides both experts and novices with a valuable tool for exploring various NPs of interest. | **[Bezier-Guided Adaptive Flow for Cross-domain Sequential Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30825v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cross-domain sequential recommendation aims to alleviate data sparsity and cold-start problems by transferring useful preference knowledge across domains. However, users exhibit varying degrees of preference consistency across domains, while existing methods often rely on global alignment objectives or coarse-grained transfer strategies. To address these challenges, we propose BGAFlow, a personalized framework that combines state-adaptive semantic evolution with user-specific transfer control. The learned flow incorporates dynamic preference feedback to adjust transfer directions, while recommendation supervision and trajectory regularization encourage effective and smooth adaptation. Conventional controllers address these effects through explicit models and estimation, whereas learning-based controllers infer dynamics from experience, yet control rewards provide no direct target for the response information retained from history, and fixed task sampling can leave persistent tracking difficulties insufficiently explored. We present ALMA, an aerial loco-manipulation framework for adaptive flight control under external arm commands. ALMA employs a contextual flight policy that adapts from recent measurements and actions, using action-conditioned prediction of future measurement representations to train its context to retain response information relevant to flight control. To expand the experience available for this adaptation, an evolutionary process uses a large language model (LLM) to analyze observed control difficulties and propose new structures of arm motion for subsequent training. In simulation, ALMA reduces position root-mean-square error (RMSE) by up to 24.7% relative to the strongest baseline, while the same policy transfers zero-shot to the aerial manipulator and reduces hover position RMSE by 79.6% during periodic arm motion relative to the real-world baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[Amplifying Latency and Cost of LLM Inference via Rowhammer Attack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30798v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) achieve high inference efficiency using optimized GPU kernels generated by just-in-time (JIT) compilation frameworks such as Triton. Triton compiles GPU kernels through an MLIR/LLVM-based pipeline. We investigate whether a Rowhammer-induced bit-flip in the CPU's DRAM can degrade GPU-based LLM inference performance while preserving output correctness. We present LLVMhammer, the first Rowhammer attack that causes LLM inference slowdown by inducing a bit-flip in Triton's compilation pipeline. A bit-flip in a performance-critical optimization pass causes Triton to generate a functionally identical but suboptimal GPU kernel. Since Triton caches the generated kernel on disk, a single successful bit-flip causes persistent slowdown across subsequent inference executions. Triton's target-independent stages are shared across GPU vendors, which makes our attack GPU vendor-agnostic. To identify exploitable bit-flip locations without manual inspection, we build Karnage, a tool that isolates useful bit-flip locations in Triton's target-independent compilation pipeline. We evaluate LLVMHammer across a diverse set of LLMs, including Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3, and Deepseek-llm-7B-chat, across NVIDIA RTX 4090 and AMD RX 7900 XTX GPUs. | **[GIFT: Gradient Informed Filtering for Distillation Augmented Reasoning RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30796v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training large language models for complex reasoning tasks relies on two complementary signals: sparse, outcome-grounded reinforcement learning (RL) for correctness, and dense, token-level distillation for guidance. We propose \textbf{GIFT} (\textbf{G}radient-\textbf{I}nformed \textbf{F}il\textbf{T}ering for Distillation-Augmented Reasoning RL), a novel method that directly addresses the allocation problem of which teacher updates to apply. GIFT performs a token-level geometric analysis, decomposing the distillation gradient into a component \textbf{parallel} to the RL gradient, which measures first-order conflict and provides a \textbf{safety} filter, and a component \textbf{orthogonal} to it, which quantifies novel directional guidance and provides a measure of \textbf{value}. This decomposition yields a principled scoring rule to rank token-level guidance. We combine this with a progressive \textbf{coverage annealing} schedule that shrinks the budget of tokens receiving distillation, ensuring that the most conflicting updates are discarded first while the most complementary ones are retained at full strength. This facilitates a smooth transition from dense, geometrically-filtered guidance to a pure, outcome-grounded objective at convergence. Extensive experiments on mathematical and logical reasoning benchmarks show that GIFT consistently outperforms strong baselines across multiple model scales, achieving relative gains of up to 9.5\% on difficult mathematical reasoning tasks. Code: https://github.com/lilifree/GIFT</p></details> |  |
| **[Agent-Environment Alignment via Automated Interface Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.21055v3)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents have shown impressive reasoning capabilities in interactive decision-making tasks. These agents interact with environment through intermediate interfaces, such as predefined action spaces and interaction rules, which mediate the perception and action. However, mismatches often happen between the internal expectations of the agent regarding the influence of its issued actions and the actual state transitions in the environment, a phenomenon referred to as agent-environment misalignment. While prior work has invested substantially in improving agent strategies and environment design, the critical role of the interface still remains underexplored. In this work, we empirically demonstrate that agent-environment misalignment poses a significant bottleneck to agent performance. To mitigate this issue, we propose ALIGN, an Auto-Aligned Interface Generation framework that alleviates the misalignment by enriching the interface. Specifically, the ALIGN-generated interface enhances both the static information of the environment and the step-wise observations returned to the agent. Implemented as a lightweight wrapper, this interface achieves the alignment without modifying either the agent logic or the environment code. Experiments across multiple domains including embodied tasks, web navigation and tool-use, show consistent performance improvements, with up to a 45.67% success rate improvement observed in ALFWorld. Meanwhile, ALIGN-generated interface can generalize across different agent architectures and LLM backbones without interface regeneration. Code and experimental results are available at https://github.com/THUNLP-MT/ALIGN.</p></details> |  |
| **[Gandalf: Flash Shall Not Die! Increasing Flash Storage Lifetime for KV Cache Offloading in LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30791v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>KV caching reduces the serving cost of large language models (LLMs), but the growing KV cache footprint is increasingly exceeds GPU and host-memory capacity. Therefore, people use SSD to expand the KV cache storage capacity with high I/O bandwidth. However, in production LLM workload, such as multi-round conversation and LLM agents, the total amount of KV cache writes significantly exceeds SSD capacity, shortening SSD lifetime from years to months. To improve SSD lifetime, a natural solution is to reduce the KV cache writes by gating the KV cache writes via historical access patterns. However, that significantly lowers the KV cache hit rate (and increase the LLM serving cost) since KV caches from different attention types might have identical historical access pattern but different future access patterns. Our insight is that, to reduce KV cache writes without hurting KV cache hit rate, one need to not only consider the access pattern, but also application-level information such as the attention type of KV cache. Leveraging this observation, we propose Gandalf , the first SSD KV cache offloading system with SSD life time awareness. Gandalf prioritize the admission of full-attention KV cache into SSD over the cache generated by other types of attention, which significantly increases SSD lifetime without sacrificing cache hit rate. Our evaluation shows that Gandalf extends the SSD lifetime by 7.3X on average, while having negligible impact on cache hit rate across multiple workload traces and wide range of hardware capacity. Gandalf design can be extended to other flash-based storage, such as High Bandwidth Flash (HBF).</p></details> |  |
| **[ProQuant: Preserving Expert Quality over Quantity in Ultra-Low-Bit Quantization of Mixture-of-Experts Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30779v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) large language models reduce computation through sparse activation but still incur substantial storage costs. We identify two key challenges: (1) nonlinear quantization errors at extremely low precision are underestimated when cross-layer propagation is ignored; (2) direct router fine-tuning may cause routing drift, and calibration gains may not generalize to downstream tasks. To address these issues, we propose ProQuant, a weight-only PTQ framework for MoE models. Prune-or-Prioritize Mixed-Precision Allocation (PoP) jointly optimizes expert pruning and bit-width assignment. It allows higher-precision experts to substitute for severely degraded ones while improving bit-budget utilization. Routing-Consistent RMSNorm Finetune (RoCo) adapts expert input representations through mathematically equivalent transformations. It compensates for quantization errors to reduce routing drift. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ProQuant outperforms existing methods across diverse generative tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/ThrowBug/ProQuant.git.</p></details> |  |
| **[LLM-Guided Transportation Hub Capacity Planning with Textual Business Inputs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03651v4)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While traditional hub capacity planning models optimize effectively for quantitative inputs, they often fail to digest qualitative business context. On a real-world 13-hub freight network in the southeastern US, our framework achieves a 2.8% optimality gap relative to the hidden ground-truth, a significant improvement over the 11.0% gap produced by the traditional optimization model without textual business inputs. This demonstrates that LLMs can serve as a contextual bridge, integrating qualitative business insights into Operations Research workflows.</p></details> |  |
| **[LLM Multi-Agent Systems Are Vulnerable to Herding and Runaway Cascades](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30769v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) increasingly operate in multi-agent systems where they collaborate by responding to others' choices, creating endogenous feedback loops with vulnerability to social influence, herding behavior and runaway cascades. Compared to humans, LLM populations were generally far more susceptible to herding behavior, and unlike humans, LLM cascades were less able to self-correct. | **[Abstract Priorities, Concrete Rewards: How Stated Preferences Relate to What People Reward in AI Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30763v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI assistants built on large language models (LLMs) are trained on human feedback and increasingly personalized to users' stated preferences. Yet how closely these preferences reflect what people reward in responses remains unclear. We compare stated and revealed preferences in the public PRISM dataset, in which 1,396 participants reported how much seven response attributes mattered to them and then rated 29,347 responses from 21 LLMs. Among responses to the same prompt, features the survey never asked about, such as response length, formatting and refusal language, were at least as strongly associated with ratings as text proxies of the surveyed attributes. Whether stated priorities matched ratings depended on how attributes were measured: participants who prioritized an attribute favoured responses from models that others perceived as strong on it, but not responses that scored higher on text proxies for it. Written feedback was more telling: participants who asked for shorter answers, even in earlier conversations, gave length less weight in their ratings, and those asking for longer answers gave it more. Their relative priorities over the surveyed attributes, however, did not predict this weight. Reasons given for choices reflected perceived attributes more than measured features. Participants also differed stably in how much they weighted several concrete features, especially length, but with a median of 6 conversations per participant, these weights were estimated poorly. The largest gain from personal information in predicting held-out choices was 0.13 percentage points, compared with 1.11 for a stronger pooled model. Code and data are available from the authors upon request</p></details> |
| **[AgentDP: Towards Automated Model Deployment for Heterogeneous Embedded AI Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30760v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper presents AgentDP, which aims to deploy pre-trained AI models on heterogeneous embedded devices by fully automating model optimization and adaptation (O&A) with Large Language Model (LLM) agents. Extensive experiments demonstrate that by fully automating the entire O&A process, AgentDP reduces entire model adaptation time by up to 80% compared with manual O&A while achieving higher task accuracy (more than 13% on average) than the baselines. ## Medical reasoning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[MedicalBench: Evaluating Large Language Models Toward Improved Medical Concept Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20197v2)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical concept extraction from electronic health records underpins many downstream applications, yet remains challenging because medically meaningful concepts are frequently implied rather than explicitly stated in medical narratives. Existing benchmarks with human-annotated evidence spans underscore the importance of grounding extracted concepts in medical text. However, they predominantly focus on explicitly stated concepts instead of implicit concepts. We present MedicalBench, a benchmark for medical concept extraction with evidence grounding that evaluates implicit medical reasoning. MedicalBench formulates medical concept extraction as a verification task over medical note-concept pairs, coupled with sentence-level evidence identification. Built from MIMIC-IV discharge summaries and human-verified ICD-10 codes, the dataset is curated through a multi-stage large language model (LLM) triage pipeline followed by medical annotation and expert review. It deliberately includes implicit positives, semantically confusable negatives, and cases where LLM judgments disagree with medical expert assessments. We define two complementary evaluation tasks: (1) medical concept extraction and (2) sentence-level evidence retrieval, enabling assessment of both correctness and interpretability. Benchmarking state-of-the-art LLMs reveals that performance remains modest, highlighting the difficulty of extracting implicitly expressed concepts. We further show that performance is largely invariant to note length, indicating that MedicalBench isolates reasoning difficulty rather than superficial confounders. MedicalBench provides the first systematic benchmark for implicit, evidence-grounded medical concept extraction, offering a foundation for developing medical language models that can both identify medically relevant concepts and justify their predictions in a transparent and medically faithful manner.</p></details> |  |
| **[MedZERO: Self-Evolving Agents for Open-Ended Medical Reasoning Through Controlled Knowledge Accumulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08327v2)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have shown promise in medical question answering and clinical reasoning, yet their improvement remains constrained by static parametric knowledge and costly expert supervision. Self-evolving agents offer a promising alternative by enabling models to improve through iterative task generation and problem-solving. However, most existing self-evolving methods are designed for easily verifiable domains such as mathematics and coding, where solutions can be checked by exact answers or executable programs. Medical reasoning is fundamentally different: it is open-ended, knowledge-intensive, and often only partially verifiable. We present MedZERO, a self-evolving framework for open-ended medical reasoning. MedZERO couples an Examiner that generates frontier medical question-option pairs with a Reasoner that solves them through evidence-grounded multi-turn reasoning with external knowledge tools. To support reliable, continual improvement, MedZERO adopts controlled knowledge accumulation, which maintains temporary exploratory knowledge and curated persistent knowledge in reasoning. We evaluate MedZERO on five public medical reasoning benchmarks using 4B- and 8B-scale base models under open-ended evaluation. At both model scales, MedZERO achieves the highest average accuracy among the evaluated methods, with gains of up to 13.7 percentage points over the next-best self-evolving baseline. Code available at \url{https://github.com/IcecreamArtist/MedZERO}.</p></details> | <details><summary>accep...</summary><p>accepted by NIPS 2026</p></details> |

In this paper, we propose Query-Conditioned Entity Alignment (QCEA), which reformulates entity alignment as a query-conditioned correspondence problem. Instead of learning a fixed mapping between entity representations, QCEA treats the textual description of a source entity as a query and ranks candidate entities in the target graph, enabling context-dependent alignment. The framework integrates semantic encoding, graph-based representation learning, and a direction-aware transformation module to capture asymmetric and many-to-many correspondence across heterogeneous knowledge systems. We evaluate QCEA on TCM--WM knowledge graphs derived from SymMap, covering both symptom alignment and herb--molecule alignment tasks. Experimental results show consistent improvements over representative baselines, particularly on rank-sensitive metrics such as Hit@K and MRR. Furthermore, downstream retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) experiments demonstrate that improved alignment leads to better evidence retrieval, stronger grounding, and higher answer accuracy. These findings highlight that alignment is not merely a data integration step, but a key factor that shapes knowledge accessibility and reliability in cross-system medical reasoning.</p></details> |  |
| **[How Good LLMs Are at Answering Bangla Medical Visual Questions? Dataset and Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18111v1)** | 2026-05-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Vision Language Models (LVLMs) have enabled general-purpose systems to demonstrate promising capabilities in complex reasoning tasks, including those in the medical domain. Medical Visual Question Answering (MedVQA) has particularly benefited from these developments. However, despite Bangla being one of the most widely spoken languages globally, there exists no established MedVQA benchmark for it. To address this gap, we introduce BanglaMedVQA, a dataset comprising clinically validated image-question-answer pairs, along with a comprehensive evaluation of current foundation models on this resource. Consistent with prior findings that report low performance of current models on English MedVQA benchmarks, our analysis reveals that Bangla performance is substantially lower, reflecting the challenges inherent to low-resource languages. Even top-performing models such as Gemini and GPT-4.1 mini fail to accurately answer specialized diagnostic questions, indicating severe limitations in fine-grained medical reasoning. Although certain open-source models, such as Gemma-3, occasionally outperform these models in general categories, they too struggle with clinically complex questions, underscoring the urgent need for top-notch evaluation method.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 7 figures, 5 tables, Proceedings of The Second AAAI Bridge Program on AI for Medicine and Healthcare, PMLR 317:1-14, 2026</p></details> |
| **[Marking the Wrong Symptoms: Evaluating LLM Watermarks in Medical Texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20462v1)** | 2026-05-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into clinical workflows, stressing the need for reliable traceability of model-generated output with watermarking. Yet, most watermarks are evaluated on general-purpose benchmarks, leaving domains like medicine, where small token-level perturbations can result in significant semantic changes, underexplored. In this work, we present the first rigorous study of how LLM watermarks affect medical performance, benchmarking 5 watermarking schemes across 11 LLMs and 7 VLMs on various tasks spanning unimodal and multimodal clinical reasoning. Importantly, we complement existing evaluations by introducing a human-expert-validated pipeline for systematically auditing medical reasoning quality, terminological precision, and induced hallucinations. Our results reveal that watermarking can induce substantial degradation across multiple failure modes, including lexical corruption, hallucinated terminology, and amplified misattribution or omission of image findings. Notably, we find that the absence of domain-specific analyses, combined with aggregate metrics that miss failures inherent to clinical text, can systematically obscure practical watermark-induced degradations. Our findings establish domain-specific evaluation as a prerequisite for the safe deployment of watermarked models in medicine, where current benchmarks can otherwise mask clinically consequential failures.</p></details> |  |
| **[DeepTumorVQA: A Hierarchical 3D CT Benchmark for Stage-Wise Evaluation of Medical VLMs and Tool-Augmented Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09679v1)** | 2026-05-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Medical vision-language models (VLMs) and AI agents have made significant progress in learning to analyze and reason about clinical images. However, existing medical visual question answering (VQA) benchmarks collapse model capabilities into a single accuracy score, obscuring where and why models fail. We propose DeepTumorVQA, a hierarchical benchmark that follows the multi-stage evidence chain in tumor diagnosis and decomposes 3D CT reasoning into four stages: recognition, measurement, visual reasoning, and medical reasoning. Higher-level questions remain independently scorable, while their ground-truth evidence chains are defined over lower-level primitives. The benchmark contains 476K questions across 42 clinical subtypes on 9,262 3D CT volumes. In addition to a direct reasoning mode for VLMs, DeepTumorVQA provides tool-interaction environments for agent evaluation, where a model can call external tools, including segmentation models, measurement programs, and medical knowledge modules, before answering the question. Evaluating over 30 model configurations, we find that reliable quantitative measurement is the primary bottleneck, making later-stage visual and medical reasoning harder for VLMs, while tool augmentation substantially mitigates this issue. When tools are available, leveraging medical knowledge and tools to reason about medical images becomes a new challenge. We further show that ground-truth step-by-step tool-use traces from DeepTumorVQA can supervise agents and reduce tool-use and reasoning failures. This stage-wise progression from recognition to measurement to visual and medical reasoning provides a concrete roadmap for future medical VLM and AI agent studies. All data and code are released at https://github.com/Schuture/DeepTumorVQA.</p></details> |  |
| **[QM-ToT: A Medical Tree of Thoughts Reasoning Framework for Quantized Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.12334v2)** | 2026-05-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) face significant challenges in specialized biomedical tasks due to the inherent complexity of medical reasoning and the sensitive nature of clinical data. Existing LLMs often struggle with intricate medical terminology and the need for accurate clinical insights, leading to performance reduction when quantized for resource-constrained deployment. To address these issues, we propose Quantized Medical Tree of Thought (QM-ToT), a path-based reasoning framework. QM-ToT leverages a Tree of Thought (ToT) reasoning approach to decompose complex medical problems into manageable subtasks, coupled with evaluator assessment layers. This framework facilitates substantial performance improvements in INT4-quantized models on the challenging MedQAUSMLE dataset. Specifically, we demonstrate a remarkable accuracy increase from 34% to 50% for the LLaMA2-70b model and from 58.77% to 69.49% for LLaMA-3.1-8b. Besides, we also proposed an effect data distillation method based on ToT. Compared to the traditional distillation method, we achieved an improvement of 86. 27% while using only 3.9% of the data.This work, for the first time, showcases the potential of ToT to significantly enhance performance on complex biomedical tasks, establishing a crucial foundation for future advances in deploying high-performing quantized LLM in resource-limited medical settings.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by ICIC 2026 Poster</p></details> |
| **[CLR-voyance: Reinforcing Open-Ended Reasoning for Inpatient Clinical Decision Support with Outcome-Aware Rubrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09584v1)** | 2026-05-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inpatient clinical reasoning is a sequential decision under partial observability: the clinician sees the admission so far and must choose the next action whose downstream consequences are not yet visible. Existing clinical-LLM evaluations and RL rewards signals collapse this into closed-form retrieval, clinical journey leakage, or unanchored LLM-as-judge scoring. We introduce CLR-voyance, a framework that reformulates inpatient reasoning as a Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (POMDP) and supervises it with rewards that are simultaneously outcome-grounded and clinician-validated. We instantiate the formulation as CLR-POMDP, which partitions successful patient journeys into a policy-visible past and an oracle-only future. Using the past information, an oracle LLM generates a case-specific query-answer pair, and the first adaptive rubric for clinical reasoning which is verifiable in the future of the patient journey. These rubrics are used for both post-training and evaluation of models for inpatient clinical reasoning. We post-train Qwen3-8B and MedGemma-4B with GRPO followed by model merging, yielding state-of-the-art inpatient clinical reasoning while retaining generalist capabilities. CLR-voyance-8B achieves 84.91% on CLR-POMDP, ahead of frontier medical reasoning models like GPT-5 (77.83%) and MedGemma-27B (66.66%) and has comparable or better performance on existing medical benchmarks. To ensure a clinically meaningful setting, we conduct a large-scale clinician alignment study, where physicians curate per-case rubrics, grade candidate responses, and provide blinded pairwise preferences of model reasoning. This study provides insights on clinical LLM-as-a-judge and clinical preference-model selection, which can inform the community at large. CLR-voyance has been deployed for 6+ months at a partner public hospital, drafting thousands of reasoning-heavy inpatient notes.</p></details> |  |
| **[Large Language Models Show Metacognitive Sensitivity in Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14552v1)** | 2026-05-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated and used in medicine, but clinical usefulness depends on answer accuracy and whether confidence tracks evidence quality and uncertainty. We developed a controlled, psychophysics-inspired clinical benchmark to test diagnostic choice and confidence behavior in a medical LLM. The benchmark focused on probable Alzheimer-type neurocognitive disorder (AT-NCD) versus depression-related cognitive impairment (DRCI). We generated 45 synthetic vignettes varying evidence strength, conflicting evidence, and missing information. Each vignette was presented under three prompt variants, yielding 135 trials. In a pilot run with gpt-4.1-nano, all trials produced valid structured outputs. Across forced-choice trials, diagnostic accuracy was 93.5%, mean confidence was 78.4%, and AUROC2 was 0.876. Confidence increased with evidence distance from the diagnostic boundary, decreased when information was missing, and remained higher on correct than incorrect trials after adjustment for evidence strength and prompt format. These findings indicate partial metacognitive sensitivity rather than globally uninformative confidence. However, errors clustered in moderate, conflicting AT-NCD cases, where the model shifted toward DRCI and retained more confidence than empirical accuracy justified. Model comparison suggested that confidence quality should be measured directly rather than inferred from benchmark accuracy or model capability alone. This study establishes a reproducible framework for evaluating evidence sensitivity, metacognitive sensitivity, and localized calibration failure in medical LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Medmarks: A Comprehensive Open-Source LLM Benchmark Suite for Medical Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01417v1)** | 2026-05-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluating large language models (LLMs) for medical applications remains challenging due to benchmark saturation, limited data accessibility, and insufficient coverage of relevant tasks. Existing suites have either saturated, heavily depend on restricted datasets, or lack comprehensive model coverage. We introduce Medmarks, a fully open-source evaluation suite with 30 benchmarks spanning question answering, information extraction, medical calculations, and open-ended clinical reasoning. We perform a systematic evaluation of 61 models across 71 configurations using verifiable metrics and LLM-as-a-Judge. Our results show that frontier reasoning models (Gemini 3 Pro Preview, GPT-5.1, & GPT-5.2) achieve the highest performance across both benchmarks, most frontier proprietary models are significantly more token efficient than open-weight alternatives, medically fine-tuned models outperform their generalist counterparts, and that models are susceptible to answer-order bias (particularly smaller models and Grok 4). A subset of our evals (Medmarks-T) can be directly used as reinforcement learning environments to post-train LLMs for medical reasoning. Code is available at https://github.com/MedARC-AI/Medmarks</p></details> | <details><summary>websi...</summary><p>website: https://medmarks.ai</p></details> |
| **[CURE-Med: Curriculum-Informed Reinforcement Learning for Multilingual Medical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13262v2)** | 2026-04-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While large language models (LLMs) have shown to perform well on monolingual mathematical and commonsense reasoning, they remain unreliable for multilingual medical reasoning applications, hindering their deployment in multilingual healthcare settings. We address this by first introducing CUREMED-BENCH, a high-quality multilingual medical reasoning dataset with open-ended reasoning queries with a single verifiable answer, spanning thirteen languages, including underrepresented languages such as Amharic, Yoruba, and Swahili. Building on this dataset, we propose CURE-MED, a curriculum-informed reinforcement learning framework that integrates code-switching-aware supervised fine-tuning and Group Relative Policy Optimization to jointly improve logical correctness and language stability. Across thirteen languages, our approach consistently outperforms strong baselines and scales effectively, achieving 85.21% language consistency and 54.35% logical correctness at 7B parameters, and 94.96% language consistency and 70.04% logical correctness at 32B parameters. These results support reliable and equitable multilingual medical reasoning in LLMs. The code and dataset are available at https://cure-med.github.io/</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at ACL 2026, main conference, oral presentation</p></details> |
| **[MedVerse: Efficient and Reliable Medical Reasoning via DAG-Structured Parallel Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.07529v3)** | 2026-04-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong performance and rapid progress in a wide range of medical reasoning tasks. However, their sequential autoregressive decoding forces inherently parallel clinical reasoning, such as differential diagnosis, into a single linear reasoning path, limiting both efficiency and reliability for complex medical problems. To address this, we propose MedVerse, a reasoning framework for complex medical inference that reformulates medical reasoning as a parallelizable directed acyclic graph (DAG) process based on Petri net theory. The framework adopts a full-stack design across data, model architecture, and system execution. For data creation, we introduce the MedVerse Curator, an automated pipeline that synthesizes knowledge-grounded medical reasoning paths and transforms them into Petri net-structured representations. At the architectural level, we propose a topology-aware attention mechanism with adaptive position indices that supports parallel reasoning while preserving logical consistency. Systematically, we develop a customized inference engine that supports parallel execution without additional overhead. Empirical evaluations show that MedVerse improves strong general-purpose LLMs by up to 8.9%. Compared to specialized medical LLMs, MedVerse achieves comparable performance while delivering a 1.3x reduction in inference latency and a 1.7x increase in generation throughput, enabled by its parallel decoding capability. Code is available at https://github.com/aiming-lab/MedVerse.</p></details> |  |
| **[PubSwap: Public-Data Off-Policy Coordination for Federated RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.12160v1)** | 2026-04-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning post-training with reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR) is typically studied in centralized settings, yet many realistic applications involve decentralized private data distributed across organizations. Federated training is a natural solution, but scaling RLVR in this regime is challenging: full-model synchronization is expensive, and performing many local steps can cause severe client drift under heterogeneous data. We propose a federated RLVR framework that combines LoRA-based local adaptation with public-data-based off-policy steps to improve both communication efficiency and cross-client coordination. In particular, a small shared public dataset is used to periodically exchange and reuse response-level training signals across organizations, providing a lightweight anchor toward a more globally aligned objective without exposing private data. Our method selectively replaces locally incorrect responses with globally correct ones during public-data steps, thereby keeping training closer to the local policy while still benefiting from cross-client coordination. Across mathematical and medical reasoning benchmarks and models, our method consistently improves over standard baselines. Our results highlight a simple and effective recipe for federated reasoning post-training: combining low-rank communication with limited public-data coordination.</p></details> |  |
| **[Eliciting Medical Reasoning with Knowledge-enhanced Data Synthesis: A Semi-Supervised Reinforcement Learning Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11547v1)** | 2026-04-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>While large language models hold promise for complex medical applications, their development is hindered by the scarcity of high-quality reasoning data. To address this issue, existing approaches typically distill chain-of-thought reasoning traces from large proprietary models via supervised fine-tuning, then conduct reinforcement learning (RL). These methods exhibit limited improvement on underrepresented domains like rare diseases while incurring substantial costs from generating complex reasoning chains. To efficiently enhance medical reasoning, we propose MedSSR, a Medical Knowledge-enhanced data Synthesis and Semi-supervised Reinforcement learning framework. Our framework first employs rare disease knowledge to synthesize distribution-controllable reasoning questions. We then utilize the policy model itself to generate high-quality pseudo-labels. This enables a two-stage, intrinsic-to-extrinsic training paradigm: self-supervised RL on the pseudo-labeled synthetic data, followed by supervised RL on the human-annotated real data. MedSSR scales model training efficiently without relying on costly trace distillation. Extensive experiments on Qwen and Llama demonstrate that our method outperforms existing methods across ten medical benchmarks, achieving up to +5.93% gain on rare-disease tasks. Our code is available at https://github.com/tdlhl/MedSSR.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ACL 2026 as a Findings paper</p></details> |
